Town Management Study Committee
Members of the Town Management Study Committee are Melissa Cobb, Chairman, Diane Allen, John
Floros, Gail Wolejko, and James Van Buren, replacing Walter Keeler. This Committee was established in 1983
and charged with the task of investigating the establishment of a Selectmen-Town Manager form of
government. The past three years have resulted in the acceptance of Chapter 41, Section 23A of the General
Laws, establishing the position of Executive Secretary.
Based on the results of our survey, last year's Committee recommended that the Town form a Charter
Commission. The first step in this process was obtaining signatures of 15% of the registered voters at the last
state election. A total of 751 signatures were thus required and on October 31, 1985, the Board of Registrars
certified 828 signatures on petitions which had been circulated throughout the town. This enables the
question, "Shall a commission be elected to frame a charter for the Town of Lunenburg?" to be placed on the
1986 ballot. Names of those desiring to run for the elective position of member of the Charter Commission will
also be placed on the ballot. If the question passes, the nine candidates receiving the highest number of votes
are then elected to form the Charter. By state law, the Commission must be funded with a minimum of $2,000
and must complete its Charter Proposal within eighteen months. A review of similar-sized towns by the
Executive Office of Communities and Development indicates realistic expenses in the ten to fifteen thousand
dollar range. The Charter Commission's final report will be placed on the next regular municipal ballot after
the final report is completed.
We recommend a Charter Commission consider the following issues and/or changes in town
government:
- enlarging the Board of Selectmen;
- establishing the strength and nature of a Town Manager/Administrator position
- possibly separating the Board of Health from the Board of Selectmen;
- possibly consolidating and/or restructuring of Town departments;
- reviewing budget procedures to ensure efficiency;
- increasing communication among departments and boards.
44
===PAGE 47===
Montachusett Regional Vocational
Technical School District
The year 1985 marked the fifth anniversary of the birth of Proposition 2½. What has happened to
Montachusett Regional Vocational Technical School since 1980? Many things happened in 1985, and some
things did not happen that should have. It was hoped that three towns would be accepted into the District--
Orange, Petersham and Westminster. After five years of knocking at the door for entrance, Orange withdrew
its petition to join and is planning instead to join the Franklin County Technical School District in Turners
Falls. Unless Fitchburg decides to vote approval for Petersham and Westminster, they, too, may decide to
apply elsewhere. This would be a severe blow to Monty Tech since the School suffers from the same drop in
enrollment as is being experienced by other schools in the District. This would result in elimination of some
vocational programs offered at the School at a time when the economy is at an all time high in the
Commonwealth, and when well-trained young men and women are needed in trade/industry. This region
would sorely miss one of its most outstanding assets, a regional vocational technical school with a wide
offering of vocational problems.
The Committee, administration and staff have been busy with acquisition of new equipment,
implementation of new programs, honoring students for their accomplishments, embellishing the School
both inside and out, and generally, "fighting the good fight". The staff spent time and energy on self-
evaluation for the New England Association of Schools and Colleges.
The Superintendent-Director visited Finance Committee/Boards of Selectmen/Town Meetings/City
Councils seeking approval for Monty Tech of Section 12 (Equal Education Opportunity funds), and Section 13
(Professional Development) of Chapter 188, the new state Education Reform Bill. The two cities and seven of
the cities voted approval.
Mr. & Mrs. Daniel Proctor of Lunenburg won the drawing for this year's house building program, a project
conducted by Monty Tech each year. To date, ten houses have been built in the District, each in a distinct and
different architectural style. As usual, we are grateful for donations of equipment, supplies and materials from
businesses and industries. These materials have been put to good use in the various vocational shops.
Principles of Technology, a new science program, was developed and implemented, and has been a
source of great enthusiasm for the enrolled students and the instructor whose work made the offering
possible. This is a two-year program for Grades 11 and 12 built around the concept of a technically valid,
unified approach to the study of basic energy systems--mechanical, fluid, electrical and thermal. Grants have
been received in the amount of $213,597 allowing for opening of the Principles of Technology program,
robotics experience for electronic students, expansion of the dental laboratory technician program,
parenting/counseling classes for students, and new micro computers for science and library use.
In October, the Lunenburg Board of Selectmen regretfully accepted the resignation of Charles F. Valera
as Lunenburg's representative to the Montachusett Regional Vocational Technical School Committee. Mr.
Valera served in this capacity since 1976 and his dedicated service to the Town is greatly appreciated by the
Townspeople. On November 18, 1985, by a joint convention of the Board of Selectmen and School
Committee, Charles J. Darling was appointed to serve as Lunenburg's representative until the next Annual
Town Election.
Your new Representative reports that Monty Tech is, at this time, healthily in transition in most aspects.
The District Committee is in the process of selecting a new Superintendent-Director to replace Charlotte
Scott who is completing a commendable performance at that post. New contract/agreéments have,
essentially, at this writing been agreed upon for both our teachers and custodians. Mr. Darling goes on to state
that the District Committee itself is comprised of excellent representatives, and in general "keeps the ship on
course". The buildings and facilities are in excellent condition and in being a part of Monty Tech, Lunenburg is
providing a vital piece in the educational puzzle confronting our young people today. There are currently 16
young women and 21 young men in attendance from the Town of Lunenburg. Mr. Darling urges the Town's
continued support of Monty Tech.

Cable Advisory Committee
The Cable Advisory Committee was created by the Board of Selectmen in 1985 as an ad hoc committee to
advise and assist the Selectmen in monitoring the cable service offered the community by Adams Russell
Cable Services, the current company licensed by the Board of Selectmen to provide such service.
In carrying out this goal, The Committee advises the Board of Selectmen with respect to license
compliance, consumer complaints, quality of customer service, program packaging, and recommendations
for licensing and/or license renewal. To gather this information the Committee conducts open meetings
and/or hearings, examines the licensee's public records, institutes research, and solicits feedback from the
community. One effort to obtain such feedback has been the preparation of a questionnaire which has been
sent to all residents on streets wired for cable service. Results of this questionnaire will be made public as soon
as possible.
The Cable Advisory Committee meets on the second Tuesday of each month at 7:30 P.M. at the Brooks
House and encourages interested individuals to attend.
Cable Advisory Committee: standing left to right - Katherine E. Lowe, Lee DeNike, and Andree F. Lauterbach.

===PAGE 49===
Purchase of Services Study Committee
Article 35 of the 1985 Annual Town Meeting voted to have a study committee to be appointed by the Board
of Selectmen and Moderator to study a possible amendment to the Town By-laws which would require that no con-
tract for the purchase of services amounting to $4,000 or more shall be awarded by a Town Department without first
going out for bid. Appointed to the Purchase of Services Study Committee were James Hays, Chairman; Joseph
Giovino, Secretary; and David Rodgers. Each member met with various Town officials to solicit input and recom-
mendations. The Committee feels that a regimented bid process for services would inhibit Town departments by
creating more paperwork and would remove from Town department heads the ability to make timely and cost-effective
decisions. Currently, State law dictates the bidding process for much the Town does, services is an exception. We
found most department heads now routinely go out for bid on services when a significant sum of money is involved.
It is recommended by this Committee that all Town department heads use the bid process forservices when they feel
it is in the best interests of the Town, and when it will save the Town money. We feel that setting a dollar limit
would not be practical and would remove the autonomy from our Town officials to act on our behalf. Any effort to
further encumber operations by adding another level of bureaucracy would be counter-productive. The Committee
recommends that no dollar limit be set, but that each department head, when they deem advisable, seek bids for
services that would result in a timely, cost-effective method of purchase.

Town Clerk, Tax Collector and Treasurer
I am pleased to submit my third annual report as Town
Clerk, Tax Collector and Treasurer. I wish to thank the
various boards, officers and commissions which my office
works with on a daily basis to serve town residents and
visitors. 1985 proved that through continued communication
and cooperation, the public would be assured of receiving
proper services.
Lunenburg has reached a point in its history at which it is
important that an intensive, detailed and coordinated study
be made of its government. The Town Management Study
Committee was successful in obtaining 828 signatures to
place a question on the 1986 Annual Town Election ballot to
see if the town will establish a Charter Commission. Those
elected to this commission will be able to see what works,
what doesn't, what can be improved, and, after careful
research, will recommend changes. The town will then vote
at a later election to accept or reject it. This is an
important issue and should be supported by Lunenburg.
Another important crossroad is upon the town involving the
path to computerization. This is a must if the town offices
are to maintain their reputation for efficiency. As
Lunenburg continues to grow, the manual typing of checks,
calculating interest and paying service bureaus to do our
work should be eliminated to assure that employees are
utilized to their highest potential. I have served on the
adhoc computer study committee which is reviewing various
computers for town use. Because current personnel will have
to input the information that is currently entered by
Robert J. Ebersole
service bureaus, the number of personnel will not be reduced.
However, more complete, up-to-date reports will be available to the public and town boards, which
in turn will result in improved management decisions.
Treasurer
Each year the borrowing capacity of the Town is calculated so that we can know the limits of our
debt. Many projects may be borrowed outside of this debt limit such as the Turkey Hill Middle
School, and many within that limit, such as the fire truck. The borrowing capacity is computed as
follows:
EQUALIZED VALUATION (January 1, 1985)
$259,082,812.
5% THEREOF (Debt Limit)
$12,954,140.
TOWN DEBT (December 31, 1985)
766,863
LESS LOANS OUTSIDE OF DEBT LIMIT
Turkey Hill Middle School (balance)
130,000.
$636,863.
636,863.
BORROWING CAPACITY
$12,317,277
As seen from these calculations, Lunenburg has great potential to adequately fund needed capital
projects. We should not hesitate to use this debt capacity. The town has shown the willingness
to face up to many of its expected capital needs by votes to fund studies of the Whalom Water
System, Water Resources, Town Office renovations, Sewerage and Drainage, and the Lake Shirley
Acquifer.
I have remained active in the Massachusetts Collectors' and Treasurer's Association, serving for a
time on their legislative committee, and as an instructor on a panel at their annual school held
at the University of Massachusetts, Amherst. In the summer of 1986, I will be eligible to take the
exam to become a certified Collector/Treasurer. I have successfully completed the schooling, time
and exam requirements to be awarded Certified Municipal Accountant by the Massachusetts Municipal
Auditors and Accountants Association on June 10, 1985.
The second sentence of the last paragraph in last year's Treasurer's report under the section
"Monty Tech" is hereby deleted as the audit for FY 1984 was performed by a private firm and not
by the state, said firm hired by the school committee, and also that the school committee volun-
tarily returned a surplus to the local communities, further reducing the assessments.

===PAGE 52===
Tax Collector
Once more the goal of 100% collection rate for real estate taxes was attained for fiscal year 1985.
This means that either the taxes were paid by June 30, 1985, or a lien on the property was recorded
at the Registry of Deeds, assuring that the Town would be protected.
Under the provisions of Proposition 2½, the town voted to increase taxes for FY 86 as follows:
Allowable 2½ increase
$101,747.
Allowable new growth
71,950.
Fire truck debt exemption
38,150.
TOTAL
$211,847.
Total taxes levied rose from $4,069,893. in Fiscal Year 1985 to $4,281,740. in Fiscal Year 1986,
for a total increase of 5.2%
Due to the revaluation of property, tax bills were delayed and not sent out until December 20th.
Through great cooperation with many banks that process tax escrow accounts for mortgages they hold,
we were able to collect 64% of the first half tax by December 31, 1985. Considering that this was
in a period of seven business days and the holiday season, it was a very busy time. My staff is
to be commended for working the extra time necessary to get the bills out and collect the money,
including opening the office on Saturday, December 28th, so that taxpayers could be assured of
a 1985 Federal Income Tax deduction.
For the third year in a row, street betterments were assessed for roads in the Hickory Hills area.
Bills for the improvements were sent out in December and taxpayers could choose to spread the
payments out over a period not to exceed twenty years. This program was established so that the
roads could be accepted and improved without charging the town for the costs.
Town Clerk
In November, I traveled to Luneburg, West Germany,
and attended many municipal functions. I stayed with
Mrs. Renate Meins, a city councilor, and her family,
and was able to set up an informal sister city
relationship. The exchange of-culture that occurred
gave me a sense of how small the world is and also
made me appreciate our New England town and the
feeling of community that exists here in Lunenburg.
On the next page are reproductions of greetings and
proclamations from the Board of Selectmen, the
Governor, State Senate and the State House of
Representatives.
Massachusetts is the only state that conducts its
own census. All other states rely on the decenial
Federal census. The 1985 town census and street
listing was conducted using state supplied forms.
Between the poor design of these forms preventing
easy reading, the reversal of color codes, and the
City Hall, Luneburg, Germany
flimsy paper it was printed on, the census was not
well received by many town residents. However, the response rate was excellent and it is hoped
that each year census forms will be returned quickly. The Board of Registrars, which includes
the Town Clerk, endeavored to assure that the forms were completed in time for state deadlines,
even though the forms were delayed because of state and printing problems. The information'on
the street listing helps all town departments.
I have continued the schooling for municipal clerks and will be eligible for the award of
Certified Municipal Clerk in the summer of 1986. Active in State and county clerk's association,
I have gained valuable information for the town. In November, I was elected as Second Vice
President of the Massachusetts Town Clerk's Association and was sworn in by Secretary of State,
Michael J. Connolly.

===PAGE 78===
Following are those records of Births, Marriages, and Deaths that either occurred in our Town or
records which were forwarded to this Town from other municipalities in the State. Lunenburg residents
are urged to notify the Town Clerk if in-State records have not been received and recorded. If a Birth,
Marriage, or Death occurred outside the State, the same may be recorded in the Lunenburg Town
Clerk's office if certified records are presented. In some cases the Town Clerk's records are
supplemented by records of burials which appear elsewhere in this report. In order for these records to
also be included in the official Vital Statistics records, the family of the deceased should present a
certified copy of the death certificate to the Town Clerk. In accordance with the General Laws,
marriages which occur here but relate to licenses which were obtained elsewhere in the State, are no
longer included in our official Town Records.

ANNUAL TOWN ELECTION
To choose by ballot the following officers: Moderator for three years: Selectman for three years: Assessor
for three years; Auditor for one year; School Committee Member for one year; School Committee Member for
three years; Park Commissioner for three years; Commissioner of Trust Funds for three years; Cemetery
Commissioner for three years; Two Library Trustees for three years; Planning Board Member for three years;
Planning Board Member for five years; Housing Authority Member for five years; Tree Warden for three years;
Question 1. "Shall the Town of Lunenburg be allowed to exempt from the provisions of Proposition 2½, so
called the amounts required to pay for the bond issued in order to make repairs to the roof, windows, and
doors at the Passios Elementary School?" Question 2. "Shall the Town of Lunenburg be allowed to assess an
additional $101,705. in real estate and personal property tax for the fiscal year beginning July First Nineteen
Hundred and Eighty Five?"

ANNUAL TOWN MEETING
Saturday May 11, 1985
The Meeting was called to order at 1:00 PM by Town Moderator, Richard J. Bishop, a quorum having been declared present.
The Moderator noted that State Senator Mary L. Padula and Mr. Rosaire Rajotte, Selectman from Northbridge, were in attendance.
David Rodgers, 82 Highland Street, was recognized and asked that the Town recognize the years of service of the following persons
who have retired from employment from the Town: Donald Kenney 20 yrs. School Department, David Cox 22 yrs. Highway Department,
Roger Fitzgerald 22 yrs. Police Department, August Misner 6 yrs. Selectmen, and Richard Bingham 34 yrs. Fire Department.
It was unanimously voted that no new articles be taken up after 6:00 PM.
FISCAL YEAR 1986 ANNUAL MEETING WARRANT
ARTICLE 1. To seeif the Town will vote to amend Article XV of the Town's By-Laws entitled "Salary Administration Plan of the Town
of Lunenburg" by deleting the Classification and Salary Schedule voted on November 1, 1984, to be effective retroactive to July 1, 1984,
and substituting therefor the Classification and Salary Schedule proposed by the Personnel Committee and appearing in the report of the
Finance Committee, such action to be effective July 1, 1985, or act anything thereon. (Submitted by Personnel Board.) Finance
Committee recommendation to be made after discussion at Town Meeting. VOTED UNANIMOUSLY.
ARTICLE 2. To see if the Town will vote to amend Aritcle XV of the Town's By-Laws entitled, "Salary Admininstration Plan of the Town
of Lunenburg" by inserting after the first sentence of SECTION 23. LONGEVITY PAY, the following sentence: "At fifteen years of
continuous service, the percentage rate used in this calculation shall be increased to 1.5%, and at twenty years, to 2%."; and by deleting
from the second sentence of the same section the words "1% of his current annual salary rate", and inserting in place thereof, the words
"the percentage of his current annual salary applicable to his length of service"; or act anything thereon. (Submitted by Personnel Board.)
Finance Committee recommends approval. VOTED.
ARTICLE 3. To see if the Town will vote to discontinue a fund established under Article 39 of the 1982 Annual Town Meeting. Said
fund being established under authority of General Laws Chapter 40, Section 5D for the purpose of offsetting Lunenburg's anticipated
cost of funding the Contributory Retirement System. Any amounts currently in said fund are hereby appropriated to offset the Worcester
County Contributory Retirement System Assessment for the above purposes, or act anything thereon. (Submitted by Selectmen.)
Finance Committee recommends approval. VOTED UNANIMOUSLY.
ARTICLE 4. To see if the Town will vote to raise by taxation and appropriate, or appropriate from available funds, all sums of money
necessary to defray the charges and expenses of the Town for Fiscal Year 1986 and to fix the salaries and compensation of all elected
officers of the Town and any other items included in the Budget of the Finance Committee, or act anything thereon. (Submitted by
Finance Committee.) Finance Committee recommends approval.
VOTED THAT THE BUDGET BE TAKEN UP AT THIS TIME AND THAT THE TOWN VOTE TO APPROPRIATE THOSE SPECIFIC SUMS
OF MONEY RECOMMENDED IN THE FOURTH COLUMN OF THE BUDGET APPEARING ON PAGES 4 AND 5 OF THE FINANCE
COMMITTEE REPORT AND CHANGES THERETO SHOWN ON THE HANDOUT PRESENTED AT THE MEETING, AND THAT TO
MEET SUCH APPROPRIATION:
$354,043. be transferred from the Fund Balance Available for Appropriation (Free Cash);
$35,000 be transferred from the Fund Balance Reserved for Abatements and Exemptions Surplus (Overlay Reserve) to the Reserve
Fund;
$130,000 be transferred from Revenue Sharing to pay for a portion of the school budget; and that the following article balances be
appropriated:
$2237.50 from Revenue Sharing Article 35 of the 1981 Annual Town Meeting to pay for a portion of the school budget; (Tax Title
Foreclosures)
$2000.00 from Revenue Sharing Article 37 of the 1977 Annual Town Meeting to pay for a portion of the school budget; (Land
Reconstruction, Alteration of Streets)
$200.00 from Article 22 of the 1983 Annual Town Meeting; (Acceptance of Hemlock Drive)
$500.01 from Article 1 of the 1981 Special Town Meeting; (Reval. of Real and Personal Property)
$5000.00 from Article 10 of the 1984 Annual Town Meeting: (Purchase of Radio Equipment)
$500.00 from Article 6 of the 1977 Annual Town Meeting; (Emergency Measures to prevent spread of rubbish at the old landfill)
$636.17 from Article 24 of the 1984 Annual Town Meeting; (Removal of Snow and Ice from Private Ways)
$1471.50 from Article 14 of the 1984 Annual Town Meeting; (Site Safety Equipment)
$5108.88 from Article 15 of the 1984 Annual Town Meeting; (Dump Truck)
$109.61 from the Article 31 of the 1984 Annual Town Meeting: (Oiling South Cemetery Roads)
The remainder of the total budget voted to be raised by taxation and appropriated and that to supplement amounts appropriated under
the budget, the Town vote to approve the appropriation of $4202.50 from monies received from State Aid to Libraries for Library purposes,
and $9700.00 from monies received from Cemetery Trust Funds for cemetery purposes.

ARTICLE 5. To see if the Town will vote to accept the regular annual written reports of the Town Officers and Committees, or act
anything thereon. (Submitted by Selectmen.) Finance Committee recommends approval. VOTED UNANIMOUSLY.
ARTICLE 6. To see if the Town will vote to raise by taxation and appropriate, or appropriate from available funds, the sum of $6,000, or
some other sum or sums, to permit the Board of Selectmen to enter into contractual agreement with a qualified party to conduct a study to
evaluate the current condition of the portion of the water system owned and operated by the City of Leominster which is located within the
Town of Lunenburg, or act anything thereon. (Submitted by Selectmen.) Finance Committee recommends approval. VOTED
UNANIMOUSLY TO RAISE $6,000.00 BY TAXATION.
ARTICLE 7. To see if the Town will vote to hear a report from the Special Water Resource Committee appointed pursuant to Article 51 of
the 1984 Annual Town Meeting, or act anything thereon. (Submitted by Special Water Resource Committee.) Finance Committee
recommends approval. VOTED.
ARTICLE 8. To see if the Town will vote to continue until the 1986 Annual Town Meeting the Special Water Resource Committee as it is
presently constituted, and as charged by Article 51 of the 1984 Annual Town Meeting, or act anything thereon. (Submitted by Special
Water Resource Committee.) Finance Committee recommends approval. VOTED UNANIMOUSLY.
ARTICLE 9. To see if the Town will vote to raise by taxation and appropriate, or appropriate from available funds, the sum of $20,000 or
some other sum or sums, to conduct a search on a town-wide basis to identify a source or sources of water suitable for public drinking
usage, and the appraisal of indentified sources, or act anything thereon. (Submitted by Special Water Resource Committee.) Finance
Committee recommends approval. VOTED UNANIMOUSLY TO RAISE $20,000. BY TAXATION.
ARTICLE 10. To see if the Town will vote to hear the report of the Town Management Study Committee reappointed pursuant to Article
44 of the 1984 Annual Town Meeting, or act anything thereon. (Submitted by Town Management Study Committee.) Finance Committee

ARTICLE 58. To see if the Town will vote to amend Article IX of the Town By-Laws by adding a new Section as follows: Section 17 Any
person, before proceeding to go from door to door within the town for the purpose of bartering, selling or taking orders for any goods,
wares or merchandise, or for the purpose of begging or soliciting alms or charitable contributions, shall first record his name and address
with the Chief of Police of the town, together with such other information as the Chief may reasonably require for the purpose of
confirming such person's true identity and ascertaining the nature of the activities sought to be carried on. Upon compliance by any such
person with these requirements, the Chief of Police shall issue a written permit to such person, upon a form by the Board of Selectmen to
engage in the activity described therein. Upon the request of any person being solicited, or of a police officer, the holder shall exhibit his
permit. The Chief of Police, may, however, authorize a director of any worthy cause, to solicit contributions within the town without
having each solicitor under his direction registered and recorded. Whoever violates any portion of this by-law shall be punished by a fine
of not more than $100.00 for each offense.", or act anything theron. (Submitted by Police Department.) Finance Committee recommends
approval. VOTED TO AMEND ARTICLE IX OF THE TOWN BY-LAWS BY ADDING A NEW SECTION AS FOLLOWS: SECTION 17
ANY PERSON, BEFORE PROCEEDING TO GO FROM DOOR TO DOOR WITHIN THE TOWN FOR THE PURPOSE OF BARTERING,
SELLING OR TAKING ORDERS FOR ANY GOODS, WARES OR MERCHANDISE, OR FOR THE PURPOSE OF BEGGING OR
SOLICITING ALMS OR CHARITABLE CONTRIBUTIONS, SHALL FIRST RECORD HIS NAME AND ADDRESS WITH THE CHIEF OF
POLICE OF THE TOWN, OR HIS DESIGNEE TOGETHER WITH SUCH OTHER INFORMATION AS THE CHIEF OR HIS DESIGNEE MAY
REASONABLY REQUIRE FOR THE PURPOSE OF CONFIRMING SUCH PERSON'S TRUE IDENTITY AND ASCERTAINING THE
NATURE OF THE ACTIVITIES SOUGHT TO BE CARRIED ON. UPON COMPLIANCE BY ANY SUCH PERSON WITH THESE
REQUIREMENTS, THE CHIEF OF POLICE OR HIS DESIGNEE SHALL ISSUE A WRITTEN PERMIT TO SUCH PERSON, UPON A FORM
87
===PAGE 90===
BY THE BOARD OF SELECTMEN TO ENGAGE IN THE ACTIVITY DESCRIBED THEREIN. UPON THE REQUEST OF ANY PERSON
BEING SOLICITED, OR OF A POLICE OFFICER, THE HOLDER SHALL EXHIBIT HIS PERMIT. THE CHIEF OF POLICE, OR HIS
DESIGNEE MAY HOWEVER, AUTHORIZE A DIRECTOR OF ANY WORTHY CAUSE, TO SOLICIT CONTRIBUTIONS WITHIN THE
TOWN WITHOUT HAVING EACH SOLICITOR UNDER HIS DIRECTION REGISTERED AND RECORDED. WHOEVER VIOLATES ANY
PORTION OF THIS BY-LAW SHALL BE PUNISHED BY A FINE OF NOT MORE THAN $100.00 FOR EACH OFFENSE. SHOULD THE
PERMIT BE DENIED BY THE POLICE CHIEF OR HIS DESIGNEE, THE SOLICITOR HAS THE RIGHT TO APPEAL TO THE BOARD OF
SELECTMEN.

ARTICLE 72. WHEREAS the nuclear arms race has been accelerating for more than a third of a century, draining the world's resources
and presenting humanity with the ever-mounting threat of nuclear holocaust; and WHEREAS the use or threatened use of nuclear
weapons if repugnant to us; and WHEREAS recent elections demonstrated that the overwhelming majority of Americans favor a freeze
and reductions in the levels of nuclear arms, as do the peoples of other lands; and WHEREAS, the United States, as a leading producer of
nuclear weapons, can take the lead in a process of universal and multinational retreat from the brink of nuclear holocaust; and WHEREAS,
an emphatic expression of feelings on the part of private citizens can help initiate such steps by the United States and other nuclear
weapons power; NOW THEREFORE be it known that we the undersigned declare LUNENBURG to be a NUCLEAR FREE ZONE.
LUNENBURG is off limits to the design, testing, production and development of nuclear weapons; no other activities supporting the
development of nuclear weapons are condoned. WE renounce the use or threatened use of nuclear weapons in our name or on our behalf.
In return, we ask to be taken off the target lists of any governments or organizations producing or contemplating the production of nuclear
weapons. (Submitted by Petition.) DEFEATED.

Special Town Meeting - December 4, 1985
The meeting was called to order at 7:30 PM by Town Moderator, Richard J. Bishop, a quorum having been declared present.
The meeting pledged allegience to the United States flag.
Town Clerk, Robert J. Ebersole read greetings to the Town from the Mayor of Luneburg, Germany.
GREETINGS TO THE TOWN, FROM THE MAYOR OF LUNEBURG, GERMANY:
November 10, 1985
Dear Mr. Ebersole.
I was very pleased with your visit and want to thank you for the greetings you presented.
During your stay in our city you had the opportunity to visit our administration several times and also were able to observe some of the
institutions of city government and their work.
I thank you for showing so much interest and hope that you return to your town with a lot of good impressions.
Be assured that you and the citizens of Lunenburg, Mass. will always be honored guests.
H. Nickel Oberbuergermeister

VOTED 183 YES, 2 NO, (2/3 VOTE REQUIRED 122) TO AMEND THE PROTECTIVE BY-LAWS TO PERMIT TOWN HOUSES IN
PLANNED RESIDENTIAL AREAS, BY AMENDING SECTION II DEFINITIONS, SECTION IV B PERMISSABLE USES BY SPECIAL
PERMIT, AND SECTION B. DIMENSIONAL REQUIREMENTS AS FOLLOWS:
BY ADDING SECTION II A NEW SECTION AA TO READ AS FOLLOWS:
AA. PLANNED RESIDENTIAL AREA:
A UNIFIED DEVELOPMENT, INCLUDING ONE OR MORE OF RESIDENTIAL BUILDING TYPES, UNDERTAKEN IN
ACCORDANCE WITH AN OVERALL PLAN, INCORPORATING A CONSISTENT ARCHITECTURAL CONCEPT AND
INCORPORATING THE PRESERVATION OF NATURAL AREAS WITHIN THE DEVELOPMENT.
AND BY ADDING A NEW SECTION IV B 3 AS FOLLOWS:
3. USES PERMISSIBLE BY SPECIAL PERMIT GRANTED BY THE PLANNING BOARD:
IN A RESIDENCE A, RESIDENCE B, OUR OUTLYING DISTRICT, THE PLANNING BOARD MAY AUTHORIZE, IN A SPECIFIC
CASE, BY SPECIAL PERMIT AND SUBJECT TO THE APPROVAL OF A SITE PLAN IF REQUIRED BY TOWN BY-LAW AND IN
ACCORDANCE WITH THE PROVISIONS OF SECTION V., ANY OF THE FOLLOWING ADDITIONAL USES. THE PROVISIONS OF
THIS SECTION IV B 3 SECTION V.D. AND SECTION VI.E. MAY BE MODIFIED BY APPLICATION TO THE PLANNING BOARD,
WHO IN THIS INSTANCE SHALL HEAR AND DETERMINE THE APPLICATION FOR SAID MODIFICATION.
92
===PAGE 95===
a. PLANNED RESIDENTIAL AREA, WHICH MAY INCLUDE:
1.) DETACHED ONE-FAMILY DWELLINGS OR TWO FAMILY DWELLING
2.) TOWN HOUSES
3.) ACCESSORY USES AND RECREATION FACILITIES FOR THE USE OF THE RESIDENTS OF THE AREA ONLY TO
INCLUDE GOLF COURSE, TENNIS COURTS, JOGGING TRAILS, SWIMMING POOLS AND SIMILAR OUTDOOR
FACILITIES: A COMMUNITY BUILDING NOT TO EXCEED FIVE (5) PERCENT OF THE TOTAL FLOOR AREA OF THE
RESIDENTIAL UNITS, PARKING AREAS AND GARAGES, STORAGE SHEDS, CABANAS, DETACHED FIREPLACES AND
SIMILAR FACILITIES FOR USE BY THE RESIDENTS OF THE PLANNED RESIDENTIAL AREA, BUT NOT INCLUDING HOME
OCCUPATIONS, TAKING OF BOARDERS OR LODGERS, RENTING OF ROOMS OR PROFESSIONAL OFFICES.
AND BY ADDING TO SECTION V A NEW SECTION D AS FOLLOWS:
D. PLANNED RESIDENTIAL AREA
IN RESIDENCE A, RESIDENCE B, OR OUTLYING DISTRICT, THE FOLLOWING DIMENSIONAL REGULATIONS SHALL APPLY
TO ANY GRANT OF A SPECIAL PERMIT FOR PLANNED RESIDENTIAL AREA AS MAY BE AUTHORIZED BY THE PLANNING
BOARD PURSUANT TO SECTION VIII,
1. INTENSITY
a. THE MINIMUM AREA OF ANY PLANNED RESIDENTIAL AREA SHALL NOT BE LESS THAN FIVE (5) ACRES, AND THAT
NOT MORE THAN TEN (10) PERCENT OF THIS MINIMUM AREA SHALL CONSIST OF WETLANDS OR LAND UNDER
WATER OR LAND WITH A SLOPE IN EXCESS OF FIFTEEN (15) PERCENT.
b. IN ADDITION, THERE SHALL BE PROVIDED FOR EACH DWELLING UNIT WITHIN THE PLANNED RESIDENTIAL AREA AN
AREA EQUIVALENT TO THE LOT AREA REQUIRED WITHIN THE DISTRICT FOR A DETACHED ONE-FAMILY DWELLING.
2. DIMENSIONAL REGULATIONS
a. A PLANNED RESIDENTIAL AREA SHALL HAVE A MINIMUM FRONTAGE ON A PUBLIC WAY AND/OR A WAY APPROVED
BY THE PLANNING BOARD UNDER THE SUBDIVISION CONTROL LAW, EQUIVALENT TO FIFTY (50) FEET FOR EACH
DWELLING UNIT CONTAINED WITHIN THE PLANNED DEVELOPMENT. THE REQUIREMENTS OF THIS NEW SECTION
MAY BE MODIFIED IF REVIEW DESIGNATES ADEQUATE ACCESS IS PROVIDED AND WILL NOT DEROGATE FROM THE
INTENT OF SECTION V.D. PLANNED RESIDENTIAL AREA.
b. NO BUILDING WITHIN A PLANNED RESIDENTIAL AREA SHALL CONTAIN MORE THAN FOUR (4) TOWN HOUSE UNITS.
C. NO BUILDING WITHIN A PLANNED RESIDENTIAL AREA SHALL EXCEED TWENTY-EIGHT (28) FEET IN HEIGHT, SAID
HEIGHT TO BE DETERMINED BY THE VERTICAL DISTANCE BETWEEN THE AVERAGE FINISHED GRADE OF THE
GROUND ADJOINING THE BUILDING TO THE HIGHEST POINT OF THE ROOF BEAMS.
d. NO BUILDING OR STRUCTURE SHALL BE LOCATED CLOSER THAN ONE HUNDRED (100) FEET FROM THE CENTER
LINE OF ANY PUBLIC WAY OR OTHER WAY UTILIZED TO MEET THE FRONTAGE REQUIRMENTS OF THIS SECTION, OR
WITHIN SEVENTY-FIVE (75) FEET FROM THE CENTER LINE OF ANY INTERIOR STREET WAY, OR DRIVEWAY.
e. NO BUILDING OR STRUCTURE SHALL BE LOCATED WITHIN AREAS WHICH ARE REQUIRED TO BE MAINTAINED IN A
NATURAL STATE.
f. NO BUILDING OR STRUCTURE SHALL BE LOCATED WITHIN FIFTY (50) FEET OF A PROPERTY OR LOT LINE.
g. IF THERE IS MORE THAN ONE BUILDING CONTAINING DWELLING UNITS ON A SINGLE LOT, THERE SHALL BE A
MINIMUM OF FIFTY (50) FEET BETWEEN SUCH BUILDINGS.
h. EACH DWELLING UNIT SHALL HAVE AT LEAST TWO (2) SIDES WITH FULL EXPOSURES, AND SHALL HAVE TWO (2)
SEPARATE EXITS.
I. NO FLOOR, EXCEPT UNFINISHED BASEMENT, OF A DWELLING UNIT SHALL BE LOCATED BENEATH THE AVERAGE
FINISHED GRADE OF THE GROUND ADJOINING THE BUILDING.
3. DESIGN
a. BUILDINGS SHALL BE OF AN ARCHITECTURAL STYLE WHICH IS COMPATIBLE WITH THE PREVAILING STYLE IN THE
AREA IN WHICH THE PLANNED RESIDENTIAL AREA IS LOCATED AND SHALL BE CONSISTENT WITH OTHER
BUILDINGS WITHIN THE PLANNED RESIDENTIAL AREA.
b. BUILDINGS, OPEN SPACES, DRIVEWAYS, PARKING AREAS, AND OTHER DEVELOPMENT FEATURES SHALL BE
LOCATED AND DESIGNED IN A MANNER WHICH CONFORMS TO THE EXISTING NATURAL TERRAIN OF THE SITE.
c. BUILDING PLACEMENT WHICH MAKES MAXIMUM USE OF SOLAR ENERGY SHALL BE ENCOURAGED.
d. ALL EXISTING OR PROPOSED UTILITIES SHALL BE INSTALLED UNDERGROUND AT THE TIME OF INITIAL
CONSTRUCTION. EACH TOWN HOUSE UNIT SHALL BE EQUIPPED WITH APPROVED FIRE PROTECTION RESIDENTIAL
SPRINKLER SYSTEM, IN ACCORD WITH THE LUNENBURG FIRE DEPARTMENT APPROVAL. EACH DWELLING SHALL
HAVE ON THE EXTERIOR A HORN AND LIGHT THAT IS CONNECTED AND WILL ACTIVATE WITH THE FIRE ALARM
PROTECTIVE DEVICES.
e. LIGHTING FACILITIES, WHETHER PLACED ALONG SERVICE DRIVES, IN PARKING AREAS OR ON THE EXTERIOR OF
BUILDINGS, SHALL BE SO ARRANGED AND SHIELDED THAT THEY DO NOT UNREASONABLY DISTRACT THE
OCCUPANTS OF THE BUILDINGS OR SHINE DIRECTLY UPON ABUTTING PROPERITES AND/OR PUBLIC WAYS. IN NO
CASE SHALL ILLUMINATION UPON THE WINDOW SURFACE OF ANY BUILDINGS USED FOR DWELLING PURPOSES
EXCEED 0.5 FOOT CANDLES.
f. PROVISIONS SHALL BE MADE FOR THE STORAGE, COLLECTION AND REMOVAL OF GARBAGE. ALL NECESSARY
FACILITIES SHALL BE APPROPRIATELY SCREENED.
g. THERE SHALL BE ONE ENTRANCE ROAD AND ONE EXIT ROAD TO EACH PLANNED RESIDENTIAL AREA, UNLESS A
DIVIDED ENTRANCE-EXIT ROAD IS APPROVED BY THE PLANNING BOARD.
h. ALL INTERIOR ROADS AND DRIVES SHALL BE DESIGNATED AND CONSTRUCTED TO THE STANDARDS OF THE
PLANNING BOARD AS CONTAINED IN THE RULES AND REGULATIONS GOVERNING THE SUBDIVISION OF LAND.
93
===PAGE 96===
4. OFF-STREET PARKING
OFF-STREET PARKING SHALL BE PROVIDED IN ACCORDANCE WITH THE PROVISIONS OF SECTION IV. EXCEPT AS
FOLLOWS:
a. ALL PARKING SPACES, INCLUDING ANY WHICH MAY BE IN EXCESS OF THOSE REQUIREMENTS, SHALL BE LOCATED
A MINIMUM OF SEVENTY-FIVE (75) FEET FROM THE CENTER LINE OF ANY PUBLIC WAYS OR WAYS UTILIZED TO MEET
THE FRONTAGE REQUIREMENTS OF THIS SECTION.
b. UNLESS IN AN ACCESSORY GARAGE WITHIN THE STRUCTURE, NO PARKING SPACE SHALL BE LOCATED CLOSER
THAN TWENTY-FIVE (25) FEET FROM A BUILDING USED FOR DWELLING PURPOSE
C. ALL REQUIRED PARKING SPACES SHALL BE PROVIDED WITHIN THREE HUNDRED (300) FEET OF THE DWELLING
UNITS WHICH THEY ARE REQUIRED TO SERVE.
d. THE PROVISIONS OF SECTION VI.D.3.d. SHALL NOT APPLY TO ENTRANCES AND/OR DRIVEWAYS WITH A PLANNED
RESIDENTIAL AREA.
5. OPEN SPACE AND LANDSCAPING
a. ALL AREAS NOT COVERED BY PAVEMENT, CURBING, BUILDINGS AND/OR STRUCTURES INCLUDING SUCH
FACILITIES AS PLAYING AREAS FOR COURT GAMES, SWIMMING POOLS, AND PLAZAS, SHALL BE LANDSCAPED WITH
GRASS, SCRUBBERY, TREES, FLOWERS, OR GROUND COVERS INDIGENOUS TO THE AREA. ALSO ALONG LENGTH OF
EACH EXTERIOR WALL OF EACH PRINCIPAL BUILDING, THERE SHALL BE A LANDSCAPED AREA WITH BUSHES,
SHRUBS, OR FLOWERS INDIGENOUS TO THE AREA.
b. AN AREA EQUIVALENT TO ONE-HALF (½) OF THE MINIMUM AREA REQUIRED BY PARAGRAPH 1.b OF THIS SECTION
SHALL BE LEFT SUBSTANTIALLY IN IT'S NATURAL STATE.
6. OWNERSHIP AND MAINTENANCE
a. THE AREA LEFT SUBSTANTIALLY IN ITS NATURAL STATE SHALL BE PLACED IN AN OWNERSHIP WHICH SHALL
PROVIDE FOR ITS PERMANENT RETENTION AND MAINTENANCE. THE MANNER OF OWNERSHIP, USE AND
MAINTENANCE OF SUCH PERMANENT NATURAL AREA SHALL BE DETERMINED BY THE AGREEMENT OF THE
APPLICANT, THE OWNER, AND THE PLANNING BOARD. THE AGREEMENT, DULY EXECUTED IN A FORM SUITABLE
FOR RECORDING BY THE OWNER OR OWNERS OF SUCH NATURAL AREA, SHALL PROVIDE THAT, IN THE EVENT THE
PLANNING BOARD SHALL GRANT A SPECIAL PERMIT UNDER THIS SECTION, SUCH PERMANENT NATURAL AREA
SHALL BE OWNED BY A NON-PROFIT ORGANIZATION OF WHICH THE PRESERVATION OF NATURAL AREAS, OR A
CORPORATION OR TRUST OWNED OR TO BE OWNED IN COMMON BY THE OWNERS OF THE DWELLING UNITS
WITHIN THE DEVELOPMENT IN WHICH THE OWNERSHIP OF THE NATURAL AREA RUNS WITH THE TITLE TO THE
DWELLING UNITS AND IS NOT SEPARATELY ALIENABLE.
b. SUCH NATURAL AREA SHALL BE KEPT IN AN OPEN AND NATURAL STATE, AND SHALL NOT BE BUILT UPON FOR
RESIDENTIAL USE, FOR WALKWAYS, DRIVEWAYS AND/OR PARKING.
C. SUCH NATURAL AREAS SHALL BE SUBJECT TO PERMANENT RESTRICTIONS AS AGREED UNDER SECTION 6a.
d. AN ORGANIZATION, CORPORATION OR TRUST OWNED OR TO BE OWNED IN COMMON BY THE OWNERS OF THE
DWELLING UNITS WITHIN THE DEVELOPMENT IN FORM APPROVED BY THE PLANNING BOARD SHALL BE
RESPONSIBLE FOR THE MAINTENANCE OF ALL COMMON AREAS, NOT OTHERWISE PROVIDED IN ACCORD WITH
PARAGRAPH 6a, INCLUDING, BUT NOT LIMITED TO LIGHTING, PLOWING, ROADWAY, SIDEWALKS, RECREATION
FACILITIES AND ACCESSORY STRUCTURES.
ARTICLE 11. To see if the Town will vote to amend Section IV B. 1.b. of the Protective By-law relating to accessory apartments by
substituting new wording and deleting Section IV B. 2.t., or act anything thereon. The wording is contained in the report of the Planning
Board, dated November 18, 1985, posted in the usual posting places.
UNANIMOUSLY VOTED TO AMEND THE PROTECTIVE BY-LAW RELATING TO ACCESSORY APARTMENTS BY AMENDING IVB1.
BY DELETING THE PRESENT SECTION b., AND b.2.t. AND INSERTING IN PLACE THEREOF THE FOLLOWING NEW SECTION b.:
b. AN ACCESSORY HOUSING UNIT MAY BE ATTACHED TO A PRIMARY DWELLING PROVIDED THE PRIMARY
DWELLING UNIT IS ON A LOT OF 40,000 SQUARE FEET OR MORE IN RESIDENCE A OR OUTLYING DISTRICTS, OR OF
80,000 SQUARE FEET OR MORE IN THE RESIDENCE B DISTRICTS,
AND FURTHER PROVIDING THAT:
1. THE BUILDING HAS AT LEAST TWELVE HUNDRED (1200) SQUARE FEET.
2. THE ENTIRE STRUCTURE USED FOR DWELLINGS SHALL NOT OCCUPY MORE THAN FIFTY (50) PERCENT OF THE LOT
AREA.
3. THERE IS AT LEAST ONE (1) OFF STREET PARKING SPACE FOR EACH BEDROOM OR EFFICIENCY APARTMENT IN THE
CONVERTED PORTION OF THE STRUCTURE, WHICH SPACE SHALL TO BE PROVIDED IN THE FRONT OR SIDE YARD.
4. THERE IS PROVISION FOR SCREENING BY FENCING OR LANDSCAPING OF OUTSIDE STORAGE AREAS.
5. NO UNIT SHALL HAVE A FLOOR AREA OF LESS THAN FIVE HUNDRED (500) SQUARE FEET PLUS ONE HUNDRED (100)
SQUARE FEET FOR EACH BEDROOM OVER ONE (1).
6. THE FLOOR AREA OF THE NEWLY CREATED UNIT (S) SHALL NOT BE MORE THAN THIRTY (30) PERCENT OF THE
NORMALLY HABITABLE FLOOR AREA EXCLUDING GARAGE, UNFINISHED ATTIC AND CRAWL SPACE, AND OTHER
NORMALLY UNHABITABLE FLOOR AREA OF THE PRINCIPAL DWELLING UNIT, AFTER CONVERSION.
7. EACH UNIT SHALL BE A COMPLETE AND INDEPENDENT HOUSEKEEPING UNIT, CONTAINING A BATHROOM AND
KITCHEN OR KITCHENETTE AND SHALL HAVE A SEPARATE ENTRANCE
8. THE EXTERIOR APPEARANCE OF THE STRUCTURE SHALL NOT BE ALTERED EXCEPT FOR:
a) STAIRWAYS AND EXITS REQUIRED BY LAW, WHICH SHALL BE IN THE REAR OF THE BUILDING.
b) RESTORATION CONSISTENT WITH THE ORIGINAL ARCHITECTURE OF THE STRUCTURE
9. ONE OF THE UNITS SHALL BE OCCUPIED BY THE OWNER OF THE PROPERTY.
94
===PAGE 97===
10. IF THE SECOND UNIT IS DISCONTINUED AND INTEGRATED INTO THE ORIGINAL STRUCTURE DESIGN, THE OWNER
SHALL NOTIFY THE INSPECTOR OF BUILDINGS IN WRITING.
11. NO PERMIT FOR ACCESSORY HOUSING GRANTED HEREUNDER SHALL TAKE EFFECT SOONER THAN ONE (1) YEAR AFTER
OCCUPANCY OF THE PRIMARY DWELLING, NOR UNTIL THE OWNER/PETITIONER RECORDS THE AUTHORIZATION IN THE
APPLICABLE REGISTRY OF DEEDS AT HIS OWN EXPENSE.
12. ALL PERMITS FOR ACCESSORY HOUSING MUST BE SECURED BEFORE ANY CONSTRUCTION IS UNDERTAKEN.
ARTICLE 12. To see if the Town will vote to amend the Protective By-law relative to the definition of lot frontage and lot width and
the requirements for same, or act anything thereon. The wording is contained in the report of the Planning Board, dated November 18,
1985, posted in the usual posting places.
VOTED, 169 YES, 67 NO, (2/3 VOTE REQUIRED-155) TO AMEND THE PROTECTIVE BY-LAW RELATIVE TO THE DEFINITION OF LOT
FRONTAGE AND LOT WIDTH AND THE REQUIREMENTS FOR SAME BY AMENDING SECTION 11 H. TO READ AS FOLLOWS:
H. FRONTAGE:
THE LINEAR EXTENT OF A LOT MEASURED ALONG A STREET RIGHT-OF-WAY FROM THE INTERSECTION OF ONE SIDE LOT
LINE TO THE INTERSECTION OF THE OTHER OF THE SAME LOT, WHICH SHALL BE USED FOR ACCESS TO THE LOT.
AND BY ADDING A DEFINITION OF LOT WIDTH TO READ AS FOLLOWS:
H. 1. LOT WIDTH:
THE MINIMUM DISTANCE BETWEEN THE SIDE LOT LINES OF THE LOT MEASURED ON ANY LINE PARELLEL TO A LINE
JOINING THE INTERSECTION OF THE SIDE LOT LINES WITH THE RIGHT-OF-WAY AT ANY POINT BETWEEN SAID
INTERSECTION AND THE NEAREST POINT OF THE PRINCIPAL BUILDING AND THE RIGHT-OF-WAY LINE.
AND BY DELETING THE PRESENT SECTION VA. AND INSERTING IN PLACE THEREOF THE FOLLOWING:
V. DIMENSIONAL REGULATIONS
A. LOT AREA.
1. RESIDENCE AND OUTLYING DISTRICTS.
IN A RESIDENCE A, RESIDENCE B, OR OUTLYING DISTRICT, NO BUILDING, EXCEPT A DETACHED BUILDING OF
ACCESSORY USE, SHALL BE CONSTRUCTED ON A LOT HAVING LESS AREA THAN THE "REQUIRED LOT AREA", OR
HAVING LESS WIDTH THAN THE "REQUIRED LOT WIDTH THROUGH BUILDING", SPECIFIED IN THE FOLLOWING TABLE
FOR THE DISTRICT IN WHICH THE SAID LOT IS LOCATED:

1. EACH LOT MUST CONTAIN AT LEAST THE MINIMUM SQUARE FOOTAGE REQUIREMENTS FOR THE DISTRICT IN WHICH
THE LOT IS LOCATED WITHOUT COUNTING THE AREA OF ACCESS.
A. AN AREA OF ACCESS LESS THAN 500 FEET IN LENGTH WITH THE REQUIRED 100 FOOT FRONTAGE MAY BE INCLUDED
AS PART OF REQUIRED LOT AREA AND SHALL BE BUILT TO THE STANDARDS OF A COMMON DRIVEWAY.
B. AN AREA OF ACCESS IN EXCESS OF 500 FEET IN LENGTH WITH 100 FOOT FRONTAGE MUST EXCEED THE SAID
FRONTAGE BY 25 FEET, AND MAY NOT BE INCLUDED AS PART OF REQUIRED LOT AREA AND SHALL BE BUILT TO THE
STANDARD OF A COMMON DRIVEWAY.
C. AN AREA OF ACCESS IS THE PORTION OF THE LOT BETWEEN THE ACCEPTED OR APPROVED TOWN WAY AND THE
POINT WHERE THE REQUIRED LOT WIDTH THROUGH PRINCIPAL BUILDING IS REACHED.
2. FRONTAGE CAN BE REDUCED BY 50 PERCENT WHEN IT PROVIDES THE ONLY ACCESS TO ONE DWELLING UNIT ON ALOT
WHICH IS EQUAL TO AT LEAST TWICE THE MINIMUM LOT AREA REQUIREMENT.
3. NO MORE THAN 30 PERCENT OF THE REQUIRED LOT FRONTAGE CAN BE ENCUMBERED BY EASEMENT OR USED FOR A
COMMON DRIVEWAY.
4. ENTRANCES AND/OR DRIVEWAYS TO LOTS IN ALL DISTRICTS MUST BE MADE FROM AN ACCEPTED OR AN APPROVED
TOWN WAY.

ARTICLE 13. To see if the Town will vote to amend the Protective By-law relative to uses exempt by statute and uses permitted in all
districts, or act anything thereon. The wording is contained in the report of the Planning Board, dated November 18, 1985, posted in the
usual posting places.
UNANIMOUSLY VOTED TO AMEND IF PROTECTIVE BY-LAW RELATIVE TO USES EXEMPT BY STATUE AND USES PERMITTED
IN ALL DISTRICTS AS FOLLOWS:
NOTHING IN THIS BY-LAW SHALL PROHIBIT, REGULATE OR RESTRICT THE USE OF LAND OR STRUCTURES IN ANY DISTRICT
FOR:
1. RELIGIOUS PURPOSES OR FOR EDUCATIONAL PURPOSES ON LAND OWNED OR LEASED BY THE COMMONWEALTH OR
ANY OF ITS AGENCIES, SUBDIVISIONS OR BODIES POLITIC, OR BY A NON-PROFIT EDUCATIONAL CORPORATION,
EXCEPT AS PROVIDED IN SECTION 3 OF CHAPTER 40A OF THE GENERAL LAWS AS AMENDED.
2. AGRICULTURE, HORTICULTURE, FLORICULTURE, AND VIVICULTURE, PROVIDED THAT SUCH USES SHALL BE LIMITED
TO PARCELS OF LAND CONTAINING AT LEAST FIVE (5) ACRES.
3. ACTIVITIES ACCESSORY TO ACTIVITIES OTHERWISE PERMITTED WITHIN THE DISTRICT AS A MATTER OF RIGHT, WHICH
ACTIVITIES ARE NECESSARY IN CONNECTION WITH SCIENTIFIC RESEARCH OR SCIENTIFIC DEVELOPMENT OR RELATED
PRODUCTION, WHETHER OR NOT ON THE SAME PARCEL AS ACTIVITIES PERMITTED AS A MATTER OF RIGHT, SUBJECT
TO THE PROVISIONS OF SECTION.
IN ADDITION THE FOLLOWING USES ARE PERMIT TED IN ALL DISTRICTS:
1. CEMETERIES
2. MUNICIPAL BUILDINGS AND USE.
3. CONSERVATION AREAS FOR WATER, PLANTS AND WILDLIFE AND DAMS NECESSARY FOR ACHIEVING THIS
PURPOSE, FARMING, INCLUDING RAISING, HARVESTING AND STORING CROPS, TRUCK GARDENING, AND
GRAZING, ORCHARDS, NURSERIES, FOREST, AND TREE FARMS, AND AREAS FOR HORTICULTURE AND
FLORICULTURE, BUT NOT INCLUDING FUR FARMS OR PIGGERIES, PROVIDED THAT:
EQUIPMENT NECESSARY FOR THESE USES IS NORMALLY STORED IN AN ENCLOSURE.
ARTICLE 14. To see if the Town will vote to amend the Protective By-law to delete paragraphs B.2.o. and p. in Section IV, or act
anything thereon. VOTED UNANIMOUSLY TO PASS OVER ARTICLE 14.
ARTICLE 15. To see if the Town will vote to amend the Protective By-law by adding a new Section, VI.G. entitled Signs, and by
deleting Section IV b.t.n. 5 and IV C.1.I. said Section shall regulate signs in all districts, or act anything thereon. The wording is contained
in the report of the Planning Board, dated November 18, 1985, posted in the usual posting places.
UNANIMOUSLY VOTED TO AMEND THE PROTECTIVE BY-LAW BY ADDING A NEW SECTION, VIG. ENTITLED SIGNS, AND BY
DELETING SECTIONS IV b. 1.11. 5 AND IV C.1. 1, SAID SECTION SHALL REGULATE SIGNS IN ALL DISTRICTS AS FOLLOWS:
VI SIGNS
G.
1. GENERAL REQUIREMENTS
IT IS THE INTENTION OF THESE SIGN REGULATIONS TO PROMOTE THE PUBLIC SAF, TY, PROTECT PROPERTY
VALUES, CREATE AN ATTRACTIVE BUSINESS CLIMATE AND ENHANCE THE PHYSICAL APPEARANCE OF THE
COMMUNITY.
a. MAINTENANCE
ALL SIGNS TOGETHER WITH THEIR SUPPORTS, BRACES, GUYS, AND ANCHORS SHALL BE KEPT IN GOOD REPAIR
AND IN SAFE CONDITION. THE OWNER OF THE PREMISES ON WHICH A SIGN IS ERECTED SHALL BE DIRECTLY
RESPONSIBLE FOR KEEPING SUCH SIGN AND PREMISES AROUND IT IN A SAFE, SANITARY, NEAT AND CLEAN
CONDITION.
b. ILLUMINATION
ANY ILLUMINATED SIGN OR LIGHTING DEVICE SHALL EMPLOY ONLY LIGHTS EMITTING A LIGHT OF CONSTANT
INTENSITY AND SHALL BE DESIGNED, LOCATED, ERECTED AND MAINTAINED ONLY FOR THE PURPOSE OF
ILLUMINATING THE SUBJECT SIGN AND/OR PREMISES.
2. SIGNS PERMITTED IN ALL DISTRICTS
a. THE FOLLOWING SIGNS ARE PERMITTED IN ALL DISTRICTS PROVIDED THEY MEET THE GENERAL REQUIREMENTS
OF SECTION VI.G.1.:
1. ONE NON-ILLUMINATED INDENTIFICATION SIGN NOT TO EXCEED THREE (3) SQ. FT. AREA NOR EIGHT (8) FT. IN
HEIGHT, STATING THE NAME AND ADDRESS OF THE OCCUPANT.
2. ONE TEMPORARY NON-ILLUMINATED REAL ESTATE SIGN PERTAINING TO THE LEASE, SALE OR USE OF A LOT OR
BUILDING ON WHICH SUCH SIGN IS PLACED NOT EXCEEDING A TOTAL AREA OF SIX (6) SQ. FT.
3. ONE SIGN FOR IDENTIFICATION OF PROFESSIONAL AND HOME OCCUPATIONS, OR OF THE OCCUPANT, NOT
EXCEEDING A TOTAL AREA OF THREE (3) SQ. FT.
4. A MARKER NOT 10 EXCEED TWO (2) SQ. FT. IDENTIFYING A HISTORIC BUILDING.
5. A SIGN ERECTED BY THE TOWN, STATE OR FEDERAL GOVERNMENT.
G. A SIGN ERECTED BY A PUBLIC CARRIER FOR DIRECT INFORMATION CONCERNING ITS SERVICE AT THE LOCATION.
7. SIGNS AND DISPLAYS ASSOCIATED WITH AN APPROVED STAND FOR THE RETAIL SALE OF AGRICULTURAL OR FARM
PHODUCE NOT EXCEEDING TWELVE (12) SQUARE FEET IN TOTAL AREA.
96
===PAGE 99===
8. A SIGN ERECTED BY ANY FRATERNAL, CIVIC, RELIGIOUS OR SERVICE ORGANIZATION OR CLUB, MERELY
ANNOUNCING ITS PRESENCE IN THE TOWN OF LUNENBURG AND THE TIME AND PLACE OF ITS REGULAR MEETING,
PROVIDED SUCH SIGN SHALL NOT EXCEED THREE (3) FEET IN DIAMETER NOR NINE (9) SQ. FT. IN AREA.
9. ANY FLAG, BADGE, INSIGNIA OR DEVICE OF ANY GOVERNMENTAL AGENCY OR CIVIC, CHARITABLE, RELIGIOUS,
PATRIOTIC, POLITICAL, FRATERNAL OR SIMILAR NON-PROFIT ORGANIZATION WHEN DISPLAYED ALONG A LINE OF
MARCH OF ANY PARADE, OR IN SOCKETS ALONG ANY STREET DURING A FUND RAISING DRIVE.
10. TEMPORARY POLITICAL SIGNS DISPLAYED DURING ELECTION CAMPAIGNS PROVIDED NO SIGN SHALL EXCEED
FOUR (4) SQ. FT. IN A RESIDENCE ZONE OR TWENTY-FOUR (24) SQ. FT. IN A COMMERCIAL DISTRICT.
b. A TEMPORARY CONSTRUCTION SIGN IS PERMITTED IN ANY DISTRICT PROVIDED THE SIGN IS NON-ILLUMINATED,
DOES NOT EXCEED THIRTY TWO (32) SQ. FT. IN AREA, IDENTIFIES AN ENGINEER, ARCHITECT AND/OR CONTRACTOR
ENGAGED IN THE DEVELOPMENT OF LAND OR CONSTRUCTION OR ALTERNATION OF BUILDINGS AND FURTHER
PROVIDED SUCH SIGN IS SET BACK AT LEAST TEN (10) FEET FROM ANY STREET LINE AND IS REMOVED UPON
COMPLETION OF CONSTRUCTION.
3. SIGNS PERMITTED IN RESIDENCE DISTRICT
THE FOLLOWING SIGNS ARE PERMITTED IN ALL RESIDENCE DISTRICTS.
a. ONE NON-ILLUMINATED OR INDIRECTLY ILLUMINATED IDENTIFICATION SIGN FOR EACH SEPARATE STREET LINE
OF AN APPROVED SPECIAL PERMIT USE NOT TO EXCEED NINE (9) SQ. FT. IN AREA NOR EIGHT (8) FEET IN HEIGHT;
AND FURTHER LIMITED AS FOLLOWS: SAID SIGN SHALL BE SUBJECT TO THE APPLICABLE SIDE AND REAR YARD
REQUIREMENTS FOR PRINCIPAL BUILDINGS AND A MINIMUM OF FIFTY (50) PERCENT OF THE APPLICABLE FRONT
YARD REQUIREMENT FOR PRINCIPAL BUILDINGS; THE HEIGHT OF SUCH SIGN SHALL NOT BE GREATER THAN THE
DISTANCE IT IS LOCATED FROM ANY LOT LINE; THE SQ. FT. AREA OF SUCH SIGN SHALL NOT BE GREATER THAN
ONE-HALF THE LINEAR FOOT DISTANCE IT IS LOCATED FROM ANY LOT LINE.
b. OTHER SIGNS SHALL BE LIMITED TO DIRECTIONAL SIGNS NECESSARY FOR PUBLIC SAFETY OR CONVENIENCE AND
SHALL BE DESIGNATED AND APPROVED AS AN INTEGRAL PART OF THE SITE PLAN FOR AN ALLOWABLE SPECIAL
PERMIT USE.
4. SIGNS PERMITTED IN NON-RESIDENTIAL DISTRICTS
THE FOLLOWING SIGNS ARE PERMITTED IN THE NON-RESIDENTIAL DISTRICTS PROVIDED THEY MEET GENERAL
REQUIREMENTS OF SECTION VI.G.1:
a. ADVERTISING GOODS AND SERVICES AVAILABLE ON THE PREMISES, NOT EXCEEDING ONE SQUARE FOOT FOR
EVERY LINEAR FOOT OF STORE FRONTAGE AND IN NO CASE EXCEEDING A TOTAL AREA OF THIRTY-SIX (36) SQUARE
FEET PER LOT.
b. FOR IDENTIFICATION OF THE BUSINESS, COMPANY OR AGENCY ON A WALL OR PARAPET OF A MAIN BUILDING NOT
EXCEEDING THIRTY (30) SQUARE FEET FOR EACH SEPARATE BUSINESS IN BUSINESS DISTRICT.
c. FOR THE PURPOSE OF IDENTIFYING THE BUSINESS OR COMMERCIAL DEVELOPMENT OR SHOPPING CENTER, ONE
FREE-STANDING SIGN WITH A TOTAL OF THIRTY-SIX (36) SQUARE FEET OF AREA FOR EACH STREET ON WHICH THE
BUSINESS OR MANUFACTURING DEVELOPMENT OR SHOPPING CENTER FRONTS.
d. FOR THE PURPOSE OF ADVERTISING THE SALE OR LEASE OF THE PREMISES, SAID SIGN NOT TO EXCEED THIRTY-
TWO (32) SQUARE FEET IN SIGNBOARD AREA.
e. A SIGN ATTACHED TO A BUILDING SHALL NOT:
1. PROJECT MORE THAN ONE FOOT FROM THE BUILDING WALL WHEN THE BUILDING BOUNDS ON A LOT LINE.
2. PROJECT INTO OR OVER THE PAVED PORTION OF A STREET OR A RIGHT-OF-WAY.
3. EXCEED THE HEIGHT OF THE BUILDING.
5. PROHIBITED SIGNS
THE PROHIBITIONS CONTAINED IN THIS SECTION SHALL APPLY TO ALL SIGNS, ALL ARTICIFICAL LIGHTING AND ALL
DISTRICTS, REGARDLESS OF DESIGNATION.
1. NO PERMITTED SIGN, INCLUDING PROJECTING SIGNS, SHALL BE LOCATED IN ANY STREET RIGHT-OF-WAY.
2. NO SIGN OR ADVERTISING DEVICE SHALL BE ERECTED, USED OR MAINTAINED WHICH IN ANY WAY SIMULATES
OFFICIAL DIRECTIONAL OR WARNING SIGNS ERECTED OR MAINTAINED BY FEDERAL, STATE OR TOWN
GOVERNMENTS FOR THE PROTECTION OF THE PUBLIC HEALTH AND SAFETY.
3. NO SIGN OR ADVERTISING DEVICE SHALL BE ERECTED OR MAINTAINED IN SUCH A MANNER AS TO OBSTRUCT OR
INTERFERE WITH THE FREE AND CLEAR VISION ON ANY STREET OR DRIVEWAY.
4. NO SIGN OR ADVERTISING DEVICE SHALL BE ERECTED OR MAINTAINED WITH ANY LIGHTING OR CONTROL
MECHANISM WHICH MAY CAUSE RADIO OR TELEVISION INTERFERENCE.
97
===PAGE 100===
5. NO ILLUMINATION SIGN OR LIGHTING DEVICE SHALL BE PLACED OR DIRECTED ON ANY PROPERTY IN A MANNER
THAT WOULD PERMIT THE LIGHT BEAMS AND ILLUMINATION THEREFROM TO BE DIRECTED OR BEAMED ONTO A
PUBLIC STREET OR WALKWAY, OR ONTO ADJOINING PROPERTIES SO AS TO CAUSE GLARE OR REFLECTION THAT
MIGHT CONSTITUTE A TRAFFIC HAZARD OR PUBLIC NUISANCE.
6. NO ANIMATED SIGN OR ADVERTISING DEVICE SHALL BE ERECTED.
7. NO FLASHING SIGNS OR ADVERTISING DEVICE WHICH CREATES INTERMITTENT OR VARYING LIGHT INTENSITY
SHALL BE ERECTED.
8. NO PROJECTING SIGN SHALL EXTEND MORE THAN 12 INCHES BEYOND THE BUILDING WALLS OR PARTS THEREOF,
EXCEPT AS OTHERWISE PROVIDED IN THESE SIGN REGULATIONS.
9. NO ROOF SIGNS SHALL BE ERECTED.
10. NO BUILDING OR PART THEREOF, SUCH AS A GABLE, ROOF, OR WALL, SHALL BE OUTLINED BY DIRECT
ILLUMINATION FOR THE PURPOSE OF COMMERCIAL ADVERTISING.
11. NO SIGN SHALL BE ATTACHED TO OR BE ERECTED OR MAINTAINED IN SUCH A MANNER AS TO OBSTRUCT ANY FIRE
ESCAPE, WINDOW, DOOR, OR OTHER BUILDING OPENING USED FOR EGRESS AND INGRESS, VENTILATION OR
OTHER FIRE FIGHTING PURPOSE.
12. NO COMMERCIAL ADVERTISING SIGN SHALL BE ALLOWED, EXCEPT AS OTHERWISE PROVIDED IN SECTION IV.G. 4
HEREOF.
13. NO FREE-STANDING SIGN SHALL BE ERECTED TO EXCEED A HEIGHT OF THIRTY (30) FEET.
The Town Meeting gave a round of applause to the Planning Board for their work on the Zoning Articles.
Calvin Sholl, 276 Northfield Road, was sworn in as temporary Moderator by Town Clerk Robert J. Ebersole for Article 16, 17, and 18.

NO SCHOOL SIGNALS - 1985-86
The decision to close schools is made by the Superintendent of Schools after consulting with
the Highway Superintendent and the Police Department to determine the condition of the roads.
The practice is to close schools only in case of extreme weather conditions or unforeseen emer-
gency; therefore, when schools are in session on stormy days, parents are urged to exercise
their personal judgment as to the wisdom of sending their children to school.
The following schedule of NO-SCHOOL SIGNALS will be in effect for the current school year:
TWO SETS OF TWO (2) BLASTS ON THE HORN AT THE CENTRAL FIRE STATION
INDICATES "NO SCHOOL" AT THE FOLLOWING TIMES FOR THE GROUPS INDICATED:
6:30 AM
Grades 6 through 12
7:15 AM
Grades Kindergarten through 5, including Pre-School
10:00 AM
PM Session of Kindergarten and Pre-School
Announcements will be carried on the regular broadcasts of radio stations WEIM and WFGL in
Fitchburg; WCMX in Leominster; as well as WSRS, WTAG, and WFTQ in Worcester; and WBZ, WHDH
and WROR in Boston; and WVBF in Framingham, as promptly as the decision is made.
The cancelling of school sessions requires the completion of numerous separate telephone
calls as rapidly as possible. The crowding of telephone circuits can mean that the signal center
or bus contractor might not be notified in time. Parents can help by not allowing their children
to make unnecessary calls.
The cooperation and understanding by all will be very much appreciated.

Annual Report of Superintendent of Schools
It is a privilege to submit my eighth annual
report as Superintendent of Schools in Lunen-
burg. This annual report covers the year end-
ing December 31, 1985.
As you read the reports of the other admin-
istrators, you will receive a more detailed
accounting of the events that have taken place
in the school at each level. The purpose of
this report is to summarize the total aspect of
the Lunenburg Public Schools.
Lunenburg's Pride in its School System.
All of us are aware of what it takes to pro-
vide a community with a quality school system.
For eight years, my entire tenure as Superinten-
dent, I have written, spoken on and dialogued
about the quality of this school system.
At this time, I want to step back and thank
this community for demonstrating its faith and
support for its schools. All of you as citizens
have contributed to its success. Since the pas-
sage of Proposition 21, Lunenburg has supported
its schools with increase of 8.2% in FY-83; 4.3% in FY-84; 10.4% in FY-85 and 8.5% increase in FY-
86. What these increases have provided for you is a school system which has been able over
those years to recoup many of the losses it suffered. As citizens, we have benefited by increased
value to our homes, some of which is attributed to providing the seller with an excellent school
system for his children.
Pride is evidenced in many ways. If one has pride in their appearance, they will work very
hard to look good and be neat. Being neat and well appearing requires work, effort and time.
It does not just happen because one wishes it. If a team in sports has pride, it will work with
every fiber it possesses to appear disciplined, well ordered and in control. That pride must be
contributed to by every member of the team. Consequently, every member of the team
contributes, every member gains in stature and worth.
We find the same effects happening when an entire community shows pride in its school
system. Aside from the initial self-satisfaction of helping young people grow and develop into
well-educated and law-abiding citizens, there is the feeling that as an individual, you contributed
to a community effort. If the entire community seeks better schools, better roads, better recre-
ational facilities, better town management, etc., we all gain by everyone contributing to the
process.
The increases that this town has provided its schools has resulted in allowing us to properly
staff our classrooms, maintain our facilities and provide the resources to implement an excellent
curriculum. We have been able to provide our teaching staff with competitive salaries and proper
supplies. Our custodial staff has had the resources to provide a clean, safe and inviting envi-
ronment in which to work and learn in. Everyone who works in this school system feels the
pride that this community has in its schools.
January 1985 - June 1985.
Much effort, in terms of the school system during the early months of 1985 are spent in pre-
paring a new budget. After the budget has been prepared and presented to the School
Committee, they must review it thoroughly in order to make a recommendation for the Finance
Committee. What complicated this budget process was a long and difficult negotiations involvement
with the professional staff. These negotiations were not completed by the Town Meeting and
===PAGE 106===
therefore, the School Committee was forced to settle on a final budget figure that did not include
a final settlement of the teachers' contract. The Town voted an 8.5% increase over the FY-85
Budget. Subsequent to this action, the Legislature passed Chapter 188 known as the "Educational
Reform Bill". This bill provided additional sources of monies to be made available to the commu-
nities. However, two portions of the bill had to be negotiated: The $18,000. minimum portion and
the professional development monies. As of June 1985, those monies and a final settlement had
not occurred.
September 1985 - December 1985.
After the Town Meeting in May, we set out to properly staff our classrooms and purchase
the necessary materials needed for our students. After continuing negotiations, the Lunenburg
School Committee and the Lunenburg Education Association agreed on a contract. In December
1985, the Town of Lunenburg had a special town meeting which accepted the $18,000. minimum
salary portion of the Educational Reform Bill and also accepted the provisions of the professional
development monies. The use of these monies for FY-85 increased our budget by $41,374. which
had been negotiated for increased salaries.
FY-86 Budget Increase.
Any increase in a budget should be able to be explained to the public for what it accom-
plished. In doing so, one must remember that goods and commodities that we purchase increase
each and every year. In addition, staff raises (80% of the total budget), must be provided;
therefore, much of an annual budget is to maintain the existing program and hopefully to address
a number of additional problems. I want to thank all the citizens of Lunenburg for their support
of this budget. Your adequate financial support for schools will always guarantee a high quality
educational experience for our children.
An Accounting of the FY-86 Budget.
FACILITIES:

education classes.

(stroke sander).

grade.

WARRANT ARTICLES:
The Town of Lunenburg voted for two articles to be spent on schools -
1. $125,000. for the replacement of the middle school septic system.
(This article allows for the complete replacement of the septic beds used now by
the middle school and the high school. When this project is completed, the
elementary system will be tied into the new septic beds. This project is 65%
reimbursable from the state.)
2. $330,000. for replacement of the roof and installation of new windows
at the elementary school.
(This project is long overdue in terms of energy conservation and maintenance
and repair. When this project is completed, the elementary school will have a
new roof and all the classroom windows and doors will be replaced. This pro-
ject is 65% reimbursable from the state.)
The Future.
The future of any school system lies in the hands of its citizens. The concern and repre-
sentation the citizens have through their elected officials either moves a school system forward or
it can destroy what has been accomplished by lack of direction or lack of initiative. I want to
take this opportunity to indicate to the citizens of Lunenburg, that you have continually over my
tenure as Superintendent elected concerned and caring citizens to your school committee. They
have evidenced high intelligence, a sense of dedication far beyond what is normally expected for
elected officials and a real purpose for their existence on the committee. What this has to do with
the future is that this town brings its best to the table. We, who work in the schools on a daily
basis, appreciate the individuals you elect. Your school system will continue to be recognized as
an outstanding school system because the people you elect demand it.
105
===PAGE 108===
Conclusion.
The citizens of Lunenburg have provided us with a series of outstanding school committees.
To my fellow administrators, I beg their forgiveness in the demands we make upon them. They
toil daily attempting to respond to the needs of their staffs, students and parents. They attempt
to provide the best quality they can within the talents available in the staff and the resources
they are provided. Each of them is a caring, concerned and intelligent educator. Without their
input, direction and educational leadership, we could not and would not have what we have. To
Dr. Linda Howard, Director of Special Education, I thank her for her knowledge, leadership and
care for a very sensitive area of our school program. To the teaching staff, we must once again
recognize your talents and achievements. Teaching is a labor of love and all of you did it better
than any group I know. To the students, I thank you for coming each day and making the
system work. You, in your dedication to your studies are the "substance" of the system. You
are the end product and so far, you have done well. To Bob Okerman, Supervisor of Buildings
& Grounds, his staff, the secretaries, the nurses, and food service personnel, many thanks for
the extras you provide.

STAFF CHANGES & REASSIGNMENTS:
Although there were no retirements at the elementary level this school year, a number of
staff changes were made.
With the addition of a new second grade class, Mrs. Maryjo Crowley left her position as third
grade teacher to assume the role of second grade teacher. This voluntary assignment to grade
two is a one year experience for Mrs. Crowley. Diane Riley, formerly with the Chapter I program
at the Elementary School, now serves as a grade three classroom teacher while Mrs. Barbara Costa,
a teacher new to the school system, assumes the fifth, third grade classroom position. Bob
Bourque transferred from fourth grade classroom teacher at the Elementary School to Administra- -
tive Assistant to the principal at the Middle School. Jean Weinstein, secretary to the principal,
resigned her position to work with her husband in a new business in New Hampshire and was
replaced by Diane Brassard. Kim Howard, school nurse, left in December on a maternity leave
and will resume her duties in the fall. A year long teacher exchange saw Kathleen Kelly, one of
our teachers, and Marjorie Cannavino, a teacher from Ashburnham, trade places in the second
grade.

Through the Educational Reform legislation, those schools (K-8) who choose to from School
Improvement Councils, are entitled to state funds, which will be appropriated for new programs,
materials and/or supplies, not currently funded by the school system. It is the task of the School
Improvement Council to recommend to the Lunenburg School Committee the manner in which these
funds are to be expended.

sixteen mini-terminals available to all students
in grades two through five.

a day, four days a week. Students in grade
four receive computer assisted instruction in
math and reading, for a period of twenty min-

grade levels concentrate mostly on reading

VOYAGE OF THE MIMI:
This year, grade five students are involved in the piloting of the Voyage of the Mimi pro-
gram. The Voyage of the Mimi is a program that incorporates the use of a P. B. S. video taped,
thirteen series, program, the microcomputer, and sophisticated software, to address the major
theme of the ocean in general, and whales, specifically.
Included in the fifth grade science curriculum is the study of the ocean. The "Voyage" pro-
gram integrated nicely with this component of the curriculum. However, this very involved,
intensive program integrated well with aspects of the fifth grade math and social studies curricu-
lum as well.

AMP-MATH: (Achievement Monitoring Program)
The Math Achievement Monitoring Program is now in its fourth successful year. All students
in grades kindergarten through five are now involved in this program which monitors achievement
of the designated math skills for each grade level. This year, AMP-Math processing (generation
of computerized reports) is taking place "in-house". In other words, all processing of AMP-Math
tests, as is the case with the AMP-Mechanics/Usage tests, is done locally. This is accomplished
through the efforts of Pat White at the Elementary School, and High School computer coordinator,
Fran Burns.
MATH OLYMPIADS:
Students in the accelerated math groups at grades four and five continue to compete in the
Math Olympiads. In this program, coordinated by parents Jean and Nick Storrs, and teachers
112
===PAGE 115===
Paul Bregoli and Louise Morin, students are challenged with "brain teaser" type math questions.
Students from the Passios School have fared very well in this competition honoring themselves and
their school.
ACCELERATED MATH PROGRAMS:
Twenty-five children in grades kindergarten through two are currently receiving additional
accelerated math sessions twice weekly at the Primary/Elementary Schools. The children, chosen
by their teachers as those who excell in math and who can benefit from the additional program,
meet twice weekly with Carol Baker, Math Tutor. They take part in activities using teacher -
devised work sheets and drills as well as computer-assisted instruction.
Additionally, all top math group children in grades three through five participate in a sup-
plemental math program using the Individualized Math System twice weekly during regular math
classes. Mrs. Baker and the classroom teacher
work as a team in each classroom.
These programs have been received with
enthusiasm by parents and students alike.
PLANETARIUM VISITS RE-ESTABLISHED:
One of the finest educational facilities we
have available to us is the Alice G. Wallace
Planetarium, in Fitchburg. In the past, through
school funds, Lunenburg students were offered

HIGHLIGHTS OF AN ELEMENTARY SCHOOL DAY
===PAGE 117===
The newest member of the Lunenburg Extra Achievement Program is Mrs. Kathryn McManus.
Mrs. McManus is the mother of teacher Carol McManus, and has been devoting much of her spare
time to helping the LEAP program. Much of her work has revolved around helping to organize
the paperwork for the program as well as aiding the children with their various projects. Mrs.
McManus, a registered nurse, is no stranger to helping people. She was for fifteen years the
nursing supervisor at the Woodberry Manor Nursing Home in Fitchburg. She retired in Septem-
ber and has been devoting some of her time and talents to the gifted and talented program.
Numerous other activities will be ongoing this year, incorporating enrichment, and integrating the
arts throughout the program.
This year a parent advisory committee to LEAP was established. The PAC was created in
order to insure parent input in the program and to develop a parent support/assistance group.
It is the goal of the administration to have monthly meetings with PAC members in order to plan,
schedule events, and assign tasks to individuals.
This year's LEAP PAC members are: Anne
Rivard, Sharon Yousey, Sally Moeckel, Walter
Jeffko, Carol McManus (teacher), and Concetta
Verge (principal).
LEIP (Lunenburg Early Intervention Program):
LEIP, the Lunenburg Pre-School Program
housed in the Primary school, has expanded
once again. Over the past four years, the pre-
school has grown from one section (class) with
six special needs children participating and one
half-time teacher, to three sections (classes)
with fifty-five regular and special needs children
participating, and one and one-half teachers.
This year, pre-school and kindergarten
teachers collaborated in the finalization of the
formal pre-school curriculum which is a logical,
sequential lead-in to kindergarten program.
Pre-Schoolers enjoy music instruction
with Mrs. Maribeth Ford.
The Pre-School Curriculum, as well as the
stated philosophy, goals, and reporting system,
was recently presented to, and accepted by, the
Lunenburg School Committee.
Lunenburg is one of two Massachusetts
school systems to have an integrated (special
and non-special education students) program.
The Massachusetts Department of Education,
through the Educational Reform legislation is
encouraging the development and expansion of
early childhood programs. We are fortunate to
be able to offer this program to our community.

Report of Middle School Principal
CAROL A. EATON
ROBERT L. BOURQUE
Principal
Administrative Assistant
The middle school enrollments for the school year were:
Grade 8: 153
Grade 7:
118
Grade 6:
116
TOTAL:
387
In the last two years, the enrollments have declined as a result of the smaller classes exiting
the elementary school. The last large class of 153 will complete work at the middle school in June
1986, moving to the high school. In the future, the middle school will accept in-coming sixth
grade classes of approximately 110 students each September.
117
===PAGE 120===
Jeff O'Neill, sixth grade teacher,
Donna Crisci with students
assisting student.
for wordprocessing.
As a school's enrollments decline, there are certain adjustments that must be made. One of
the major adjustments is in the area of staffing. At the present time, there are five sections of
each academic area in the sixth grade, except for reading, in which there are six; five sections
of each academic area in grade seven; and six sections of each academic area in grade eight,
except for foreign language/life skills, where there are seven. The staff is realigned each year
as the number of classes is reduced from six sections to five. This fact necessitates that certain
staff members teach two and three different subject areas, at more than one grade level in several
cases. In other cases, the middle school must share its staff members with the high school and
the elementary school. The schedule of classes represents this fact very clearly, since there is
little room for flexibility across the grade levels.
Another possible adjustment which is being investigated is that of the fifth grade being
transferred to the middle school in the near future. During the course of this school year, Dr.
William Allard, Superintendent, has engaged various members of the schools' population in conver-
sation in order to formulate a recommendation involving the potential move. The move would be
possible due to the space}in the middle school, caused by the declining enrollments. The fifth
grade move will continue to be discussed throughout the remainder of this school year.
CURRICULUM REVISION:
The strength and responsiveness of the middle school's program have not diminished because
of the enrollment adjustments. The faculty and administration view this period as one of great
value to the examination and revision of our existing programs. Regular review of the curricular
program occurs each year, with different areas receiving attention each year. During the course
of this year, staff has reviewed and revised science curriculum for grades six through eight,
incorporating life science in grade seven. Reading curriculum in grades six and seven has been
revised, with updated scope and sequence of skill attainment for both grades and with new
materials created for improving comprehension in grade seven by Mrs. Sandralee Turner and Mrs.
Donna Crisci. Writing objectives for all three grade levels will be revised before the conclusion of
the year and will be coordinated with those of the elementary school. Middle school teachers
employ the writing as a process methodology for the improvement of writing skills and are utilizing
the writing folders produced by THE NETWORK in Andover, MA. This method highlights revision
and editing on the part of the student, with frequent conferences with the teacher during the
process of completing a composition.
Renewed social studies and math curriculum were presented to the school committee and
accepted, due to the efforts of the teachers in those subject areas. Changes in curriculum occur
all the time, as needs arise in society.
118
===PAGE 121===
ENRICHMENT PROGRAM DEVELOPMENT:
For the first time this year, the middle school has a part-time position devoted to the
development of an enrichment program. Mrs. Peg Mulligan has been working with sixth and
seventh grade students in the area of problem-solving and critical thinking skills. Students have
been involved in examining problems within the school which they wish to change or improve,
such as the cafeteria traffic patterns or school entry traffic in the morning. They began work on
solving problems within the framework of the Massachusetts Association for the Advancement of
Individual Potential, which presents problems for students throughout the state of Massachusetts
for competition in elementary and middle schools. This year our students completed the solutions
for practice, with our own faculty evaluating them. Next year, our intention is to have the
solutions entered into competition on a statewide basis. There is an advisory committee composed
of parents whose children are in the program, which meets monthly to discuss the developing
program and offer suggestions.
COMPUTER APPLICATIONS:
Great progress has been made in the area of computer applications to our regular curricular
program, with the leadership of Mrs. Mary Ann Sudolnik, who works part-time as computer
resource teacher in addition to being an eighth grade science teacher. The middle school's micro-
computer lab consists of fourteen Apple Ile systems and four printers, with a growing software
library. Each grade level will have received ten weeks of instruction in the area of wordprocess-
ing in writing in their regular English classes, by the conclusion of the school year. The word-
processing is integrated with the writing as a process methodology in the classes. Instead of
composing with paper. and pen or pencil, the students use the microcomputer and printer to com-
plete their work. Each class works in the computer room with an English teacher a minimum of
two forty-five minute periods per week. The wordprocessing software which the teachers have
employed is the MILLIKEN WORDPROCESSOR. The teachers received their training from Mrs.
Sudolnik prior to the ten week instruction period with all classes.
Several other classes have utilized the computer lab, necessitating a schedule of use each
week. Math classes have used the lab for drill and practice on a regular basis. Science classes
at all three levels have used the lab for simulations, problem-solving and creating graphs and
charts from data gathered from their classes. Mr. Dick Bishop, grade six science, has integrated
the software and student materials from the "Voyage of the Mimi" into the regular sixth grade
science curriculum for all students. The "Voyage of the Mimi" involves the study of whales and
the environment of the ocean. Students actually become involved in charting a course on the
ocean, search for treasure, investigate the life of whales, using the technologies of the micro -
computer and various other equipment which is added to it. Mr. Bishop, Mr. Dick Garvey and
Mr. Bob Saudelli have rotated their science classes to facilitate this instruction for all sixth
graders. In the spring of 1985, Mr. Bishop accompanied fifty students and several parents on a
whale watch out of Boston Harbor, as a follow-up activity for the study. Our health students,
under the direction of Mr. Dale Diamantopoulos and Ms. Jeanne Vaillancourt, have completed a unit
