DAILY PROGRAM FOR STUDENTS:
Every day the middle school students can rely on the fact that they will be exposed to many
new ideas and challenges in five areas of basic academic subjects and another special area subject
or two. In grade six, all students receive instruction in Reading, English, Science, Math, and
Social Studies. Seventh graders receive instruction in the same areas. Reading in grade seven
had previously been Communications. Grade eight students receive English, Math, Social Studies,
Science and a foreign language or life skills. All students receive physical education for ninety

to achieve success at the level one sections at the middle school. The curriculum in these areas
has been coordinated with the high school, in order for our students to be fully prepared enter-
ing the level two courses.
STUDENT SUPPORT SERVICES:
The middle school guidance department assists in delivering direct services to all students in
grades six through eight. Each guidance counselor sees each student during the school year, as
well as works with students in the area of improving relations with teachers and parents. Fre-
quently, the counselor acts as the "go between" for the students in areas that seem just too much
for the pre-adolescent and adolescent. Both counselors monitor the academic progress of students
very closely throughout the year, establishing procedures with parents and students which assist
in the improvement of study habits. A major goal with this age group is acquisition of personal
responsibility for one's own actions.
Tony Falcone works with all eighth grade students and also teaches three sections of grade
eight math, two sections of algebra and one section of pre-algebra. Mr. Falcone structures the
transition from the eighth grade to the high school. He works in conjunction with the guidance
department of the high school to provide information for both parent and student, in the planning
of courses for the high school.
Miriam Light works with all sixth and seventh graders in several capacities. She meets with
each during the year at least once, and generally much more. She assists them in the area of
personal goal-setting, managing personal problems, and improving communications with parents and
teachers. During the year, Mrs. Light interprets the standardized test results for all sixth and
seventh graders in math classes. All information is shared with parents as well. Both guidance
counselors coordinate regular education services with all special education services.
In the special education area, Marybarbara Hughes, the building team chairperson and
resource room teacher, and Catherine Leger, resource room teacher, are responsible for the
management and teaching of all special education students. They are assisted by two aides or
121
===PAGE 124===
tutors in the different subject areas. The middle school resource room staff works with approxi-
mately sixty students during the course of a week, all for varying amounts of time and needs.
The microcomputer has become a significant part of their instructional techniques in reinforcement
of basic skills. The middle school is also fortunate to have the services of a school social adjust-
ment counselor, a speech therapist, a hearing and language specialist, on a part-time basis, for
students whose educational plans require these services. The coordinator of the Special Needs
Department, Dr. Linda Howard, is located at the middle school as well. Each of these staff mem-
bers work very closely with the regular education teachers to provide the best possible services
for their students.
There are two nurses who serve the middle school on a part-time basis due to the fact that
they must also serve the elementary and high schools. The complete battery of tests is given to
all students in the areas of hearing and vision. Monitoring of student health throughout the year
is carefully carried out. The nurses communicate all concerns regarding the students to the
guidance staff and administration.
Library services for the students are beginning to return to the level just prior to Proposi-
tion 21. Kathy Lowe is the school systems' librarian, serving all three schools, with Marguerite
Okerman as her assistant, and many parent volunteers. Mrs. Okerman's hours were increased
this year in order to provide more service to students and teachers. The number of up-to-date
books has been increasing in the last two years due to increased budget support and fund-raising
on the part of the P. T. O. The library is open for student and teacher use seven periods per
day, as well as forty-five minutes after school each day. This year, we instituted a twenty min-
ute sustained reading period for all students and teachers every Friday morning. We
are
committed to encouraging students to read for pleasure and to improving reading ability.
ALL SCHOOL SERVICES:
In the clerical area in the main office, Janet Melendy serves as the principal's secretary, but
she is everyone's helper. She manages a wide variety of ever-increasing tasks which reach far
beyond that of a conventional secretary. She is assisted by Cindy Romano three days per week
this year, for the first time. Together they manage all attendance and schedules of teachers and
students with the aid of the .computer, handle all the normal clerical duties that a school requires,
serve the needs of the teachers, parents and students to the best of their ability. Jan Melendy
has trained a dozen or more eighth and seventh grade helpers each year to assist and learn in the
office.
Anita LeCuyer heads up the cafeteria operation in the school, working very closely with the
service management organization, SERVICE AMERICA, as well as with the administration. The
lunches at the middle school are varied, nutritious and attractively presented to our students.

STAFF DEVELOPMENT AND
PROFESSIONAL INVOLVEMENT:
Teachers at the middle school are highly involved in the life of the school system and the
improvement of instruction in general. This year we have numerous staff serving on the follow-
ing system-wide committees: arts festival, the community relations, the assault prevention, and
the committees involving curriculum coordination throughout the system. Staff members are
involved in pursuing advanced degrees in administration, or the subject area of their choice. We
have committed a great deal of time to training of staff in the area of microcomputer applications
in writing, math, and study skills in all curricular areas, within the framework of the school.
As many teachers as possible attend conferences to increase their knowledge base to improve
instruction. Much of this is done by the teacher utilizing his or her own funds. Constant up-
grading is taking place with most teachers. Consultants serve the teachers whenever financially
possible. Through the Chapter II funding of the federal government, we have been able to con-
duct workshops on writing as a process at the middle school. Teachers frequently work on
curriculum development during the school day by the administration hiring a substitute for the
teacher(s) involved in the work.

BASIC SKILLS TESTING AND STANDARDIZED TESTING:
Each year our sixth and seventh grade students are given the Stanford Achievement Test to
ascertain their achievement in a wide variety of academic areas. Grade eight students are given
the Differentiated Aptitude Test in order to determine strengths and weaknesses and to help
place them more effectively in grade nine. All eighth graders are given basic skills tests in the
areas of math, reading, writing and listening in the spring of the year. This year will be the
last of the testing under the old state law, and new testing will commence in the fall of the next
school year. The minimum standards must be met by all students or they are required to take
intensive courses in the areas of their deficiencies at the high school. All students in Lunenburg
have passed the minimal competencies tests by the eleventh grade's conclusion. We also exempt
very few students each year for special needs reasons. All math teachers this year are admin-
istering pre and post-tests on their curriculum to measure the effectiveness of our curriculum.
The middle school teachers are strong believers in accountability of instruction.
STAFFING CHANGES AND REALIGNMENT:
This year Bob Bourque assumed the role of administrative assistant at the middle school,
replacing Jeff O'Neill who returned to the sixth grade. Bob had been a teacher in the elementary
school with broad experience with a number of grade levels. The seventh grade staff was
reduced from having six sections of all subjects to only five. No teachers were transferred, but
two seventh grade teachers instruct at more than one grade level.
Two teachers announced their intention to retire this year--Peg Mulligan and Richard Garvey.
Josephine Meloon had announced her intention last year. Marion Aldrich-James resigned in
127
===PAGE 130===
January 1986. These people are very professional and highly competent educators at the middle
school and will be missed greatly. The individuals who have announced their future plans will
be with us for another year or two fortunately. They are very valued people.
The year has been productive and exciting for the entire school community. We welcome
visitors to the school, and guests at any of our roundtable discussions.
Proud student displays her brain!
Mr. Phil Chalifoux performs at concert.
Students cheer Donna Crisci and Phil Chalifoux!

Report of High School Principal
In 1985, the high school faculty and staff
continued a high level of effort to implement a
quality, comprehensive secondary school educa-
tional program. In curriculum, co-curricular
activities, and extra-curricular program, the
high school continues to move ahead.
SCIENCE-MATHEMATICS:
The science curriculum at the high school
continues to expand and to increase in quality.
Enrollment in 1985 was the highest in five years
in all courses.
New courses are being offered in grade
nine general science, an Introduction to Physical
and Biological Sciences. This course is designed
to begin science studies one year earlier for
general students in the high school. This
course is a part of a strengthened academic pro-
gram emphasis in the ninth grade. A new
course for juniors and seniors is being offered
A. RAY PETERSON
in Alternate Physics; this course is designed to
Principal
provide knowledge of the physical sciences for
students who do not necessarily intend to con-
tinue in college science. These expanded offerings are based on a premise that all citizens today
need more knowledge of the physical and life sciences in order to make decisions as citizens and
voters.
The advanced science courses in chemistry and advanced biology are at the highest levels of
enrollment in the last five years because of favorable student response to the continuing high
quality of the curriculum. These courses and all science courses, were supported in 1985 by the
re-establishment of the high school Science Fair in February.
Over one hundred students
participated in grades nine
through twelve in the Science
Fair. Awards were presented in
biology, chemistry, physics and
earth science. Eight award
winners were chosen to represent
the school at the regional science
fair in Worcester in March. Sis-
ters Ricia and Maria Chansky each
won regional awards and Maria's
project was chosen to go to the
state science fair in April.
Mathematics enrollment at the
high school remains at an aston-
ishingly high level. Ninety-five
percent of all our students take
two years of mathematics, even
though the graduation requirement
is only one year at the present

HUMANITIES:
winning Science Fair project.
Employers and colleges currently stress
that a citizenry which does not have a firm
background in the Humanities cannot cope with the demands of our fast-paced, technologically -
oriented society. The Humanities Division at Lunenburg High School has been extremely resource-
ful. As a result, the English, Social Studies and Foreign Languages departments which comprise
the Humanities Division have all made major curriculum changes to more adequately meet the
needs of all students.
This past year, the English department continued to emphasize individualization of instruc-
tion through a commitment to a formal writing program which emphasizes teacher-student confer-
encing and the development of specific writing skills. In addition, two semester electives--one in
reading and one in writing--were implemented to provide students with an opportunity to further
refine their skills. The entire English curriculum, in fact, was revised so that a variety of Eng-
lish courses designed to meet the specific academic needs and goals of all students is now being
offered.
For its part, the Social Studies department has also made major curriculum changes. The
Civics course and the Contemporary Issues course, both recent additions to the curriculum, have
proven to be quite successful. Classroom work has been supplemented by field trips to the
J. F. K. library and the state legislature, by participation in the Youth and Government Club
130
===PAGE 133===
and by student-led debates and seminars on timely topics. Guest speakers, including Congress-
man Joseph Early and Mrs. Kitty Dukakis, have done much to interest our students in their civic
responsibilities. In fact, the essay contest sponsored by Mr. Tom Lawson and Mrs. Dukakis'
commendation of his efforts graphically demonstrated to our students how one local citizen can
make a difference. Two of our students, Steven Bruun and Cinammon Morgan, were recently
declared local winners (first and third prize, respectively) in the Townsend V. F. W. Voice of
America contest. Plans are under way for additional curriculum revision from which all students
will continue to benefit.
The Foreign Languages department continues to grow and thrive.
Enrollment
in
this
area
continues to be strong. The multi-talented and dedicated teachers of foreign languages have
made the study of a foreign language both exciting and rewarding.
A Conversational Spanish
course has been added. Latin students have
won a number of prestigious contests and
awards. Students who study French and Span-
ish have had opportunities to test their know-
lege by participating in field trips to Montreal,
Quebec City and even Spain. More trips are in
the planning stages, as are proposals to offer a
foreign language on the elementary level and to
organize a foreign language resource room which
would utilize the latest technology to facilitate
instruction.
Each department throughout the past year
has demonstrated a sincere commitment to the
Humanities in particular and to quality education
in general.
APPLIED SCIENCES DIVISION:
The high school has a long record of
successful cooperation with local business and
L. H. S. Latin students, 1985 State
industry in training students in employable
Champions in lower division certomen,
skills and then placing students upon gradua-
receive award with Mrs. Kathy McGuigan.
tion. The Business department offers an array
of courses from basic typing, through record-
keeping and advanced accounting, stenography, electronic wordprocessing skills and advanced
office practice. While it is difficult to maintain technologically advanced equipment at state-of- the
-art levels, through the use of state and federal grant funds the department works to maintain
its standards. An array of field trips and outside speakers are used to inform students of
opportunities in private and public business and office settings. The school is embarking on a
program to expand its cooperative efforts with local business and industry to continue to upgrade
the curriculum and stay abreast of the most current placement opportunities. This cooperation
from private and public offices has been outstanding in the past, and has resulted in one hun-
dred percent of eligible business students being placed in paying jobs from the 1985 graduating
class. Several other business students went on to college training. Examples of placement from
last year's class include work in banks, automobile agencies, law offices and real estate agencies.
Lunenburg High School curriculum offerings in industrial arts span a wide range, including
basic training in wood and metal technology, auto maintenance and repair, photography, printing,
graphic arts, technical drawing and energy/aeronautics. These courses offer students basic
exploratory skills, but also can lead to placement in skilled employment in the region for those
students interested. The high school is intending to expand its career orientation and placement
efforts. In 1985, industrial arts students were trained and placed in work sites which included
businesses in printing, furniture refinishing, concrete products, auto body, general woodworking
and building maintenance.
CURRICULUM SUPPORT PROGRAMS:
The high school continues to put high emphasis on the development of study skills.
Teachers of grade nine students are asked to spend several days at the beginning of the term
131
===PAGE 134===
reviewing study skill procedures. These include keeping notebooks, note-taking, homework
organization, systematic reading skills, and outlining. In mailings and meetings with parents of
grade nine students, the school is emphasizing the parents' role in establishing study skills and
homework discipline. The parent response has been most positive.
For honor students, the high school has expanded the course requirements to include summer
reading as mandatory. Reading lists for honors courses are given to selected students in the
spring. Evaluation of the summer work is conducted at the beginning of the fall term. This is
viewed as an effort to extend the student's commitment to a voluntary honors program.
For seniors, the school is developing a program of second semester alternatives to enrich the
experience. Based upon the premise that seniors are more self-reliant and able to take indepen-
dent initiatives, the school is offering qualified students the opportunity to have challenging
internship experiences outside the building during some school time. These placements are
related to curriculum studies such as child care,
journalism, political offices or private business.
Included in this year's listing also will be broad-
casting, horse farm, town offices, and banking.
Other options for seniors will include the oppor-
tunity to serve as teaching assistants in the high
school, middle school or elementary school to
participate in student-run seminars on important
current topics, or to take a college course con-
currently with their high school work. All of
these options are premised upon qualified appli-
cants, written agreements with faculty, and
periodic evaluation of the experience and the
student's performance.

RETIREES RECOGNIZED
A highlight of the 1985 graduation exercises was the recognition of retirees who had had
twenty years or more of service with the Lunenburg School Department. Each received a framed
picture of the school in which they had taught the majority of their years of service. The follow-
ing teachers were recognized by the Lunenburg School Committee and Superintendent of Schools,
Dr. William C. Allard:
RICHARD J. ALLAIN
- Upon his discharge from the service, "Dick" was hired as a
teacher of grade six in September 1956. In September 1967, he
was transferred to the high school as a teacher of science,
including electronics. After 25 years of service, Dick chose
to take advantage of an "early retirement" and retired on June
30, 1981.
FLOYD F. BARNEY
- Floyd began September 1, 1947 and retired June 30, 1981 after
34 years of service with the Lunenburg Public Schools.
During his tenure he served as a teacher, girls' and boys'
basketball coach, baseball coach, softball coach, Supervisor
of Physical Education, Assistant High School Principal, and
Principal of the Turkey Hill Middle School.
BEATRICE M. BROWN
- "Bea" began her service with Lunenburg on March 1, 1945
and retired effective June 30, 1971 after completion of nearly
26 years as a first grade teacher with classes held in the
Town Hall, Center School, Brooks House, and Passios
Elementary School over the span of her service.
GLADYS L. BROWN
- Gladys began in September 1931 as a first grade teacher in
the old Center School and remained in this position until
June 1938 when she resigned to be married. She returned
in November 1948 when Lunenburg schools went on double
sessions to teach a combination grade two and three class.
In September 1949, Gladys was transferred to the Brooks
House to teach a grade four. She was appointed as Lunen-
burg's first teacher of special education in September 1955
and remained in this position until she retired on August 21,
1970, completing nearly 29 years of service.
RICHARD J. CAREY
- Dick began September 1, 1946 and retired June 30, 1981 after
35 years of service as a teacher, baseball and basketball
coach, Assistant High School Principal, and Principal of
Lunenburg High School.
NELDE K. DRUMM
- "Tiz" began in Lunenburg on September 17, 1955 as an art
teacher at the high school for one day per week; increased
to three days per week in September 1957 and in September
1958, to full-time as Art Supervisor for the school system.
She retired on June 30, 1977 after completing 22 years of
service.
PRISCILLA A. FREEMAN
- "Pat" joined the elementary staff on September 1, 1964 as
physical education instructor. After 20 years of service,
she chose to retire on June 30, 1984.
139
===PAGE 142===
LILLIAN S. GRIFFITHS
- "Lil" began her teaching career as a junior high teacher in
the old high school (now the Primary School) on September
1, 1941. She resigned in June 1944 to be married. In
February 1950, she returned as a fifth grade teacher and in
September 1957 was appointed as Supervisor of Reading and
Allied Subjects. "Lil" retired on June 30, 1977 after 301
years of service.
MARGARET P. HARLEY
- Margaret began in Lunenburg in September 1932 as a junior
high teacher. She was appointed effective September 1,
1958 as Guidance Director at Lunenburg High School and
retired from this position on July 13, 1969, completing 35
plus years with the Lunenburg Public Schools. She had an
outstanding record as coach of the girls' basketball team
during her tenure and is regarded as the local "historian"
of the community.
M. NOREEN KITTREDGE
- Noreen came to Lunenburg in September 1953 as a teacher of
grade one; resigned in June 1955, but returned in January
1960 to complete the school year in a third grade. The follow-
ing September she transferred to grade one, remaining at this
grade level until her retirement on June 30, 1984 after 261
years of service.
ELIZABETH LAKSO
- Betty began in Lunenburg on February 29, 1960 as a teacher
of grade two. She took advantage of an "early retirement"
and retired on June 30, 1981 after 211 years of service.
GLORIA V. MacPHADDEN
- Gloria was hired in September 1957 as a home economics
teacher in the newly built high school. She completed 21
years of service before she retired on June 30, 1978.
SALLY A. McCUE
- In September 1959, Sally was hired as a teacher of grade five.
She served as Annex Building Supervisor for nearly five years.
At the time of her retirement on June 30, 1984, she was teach-
ing a fourth grade at the elementary school, completing 25
years of service.
MARGARET M. SHEEHAN
- "Peg" began teaching in Lunenburg in September 1942 as
teacher of grades one, two, three combination at the old West
School in Whalom. In 1953, Peg was transferred to the new
Passios Elementary School as a teacher of grade one and
remained in that position until June 30, 1972 when she retired
after completing 30 years of service.
SIRKKA W. SHINNER
- Sirkka came to Lunenburg in September 1959 as a second grade
teacher when an additional section was needed. She remained
at this grade level until her retirement on June 18, 1982 after
23 years of service.
BETHEL E. TAPPLY
- "Beth" began in September 1958 as a teacher of junior high
English. During her 24 years in Lunenburg, she also served
as a high school guidance counselor, Director of Guidance,
and as the school system's School Psychologist. She retired
on June 18, 1982.

===PAGE 143===
IN MEMORY OF
While serving as summer school director,
Harry Angevine was stricken at Lunenburg High
School, removed to Burbank Hospital in the
ambulance where he died a short time later on
July 25, 1985.
Harry began his service with the Lunenburg
School Department on September 1, 1965 as a
teacher of junior high English, replacing a
teacher who was transferred elsewhere within
the system. Effective September 1, 1966, Harry
was appointed as a reading teacher and upon
being certified in reading in October 1968, he
became a reading specialist. At the time of his
death he was serving as a teacher of English and
reading at the high school level. He served as
high school audio-visual coordinator from Septem-
ber 1972 to June 1974 and had served as Tri-
Community Summer School Director since the
summer of 1983. He also served as instructor of
HARRY E. ANGEVINE
several after-school activities at the secondary
level over the years of his tenure.
Harry graduated from Notre Dame High School in Fitchburg in 1960 and from Fitchburg State
College in 1965 with a bachelor of science in education degree and received his master's degree in
August 1970 from Fitchburg State College.
He was a member of the Leominster Knights of Columbus, Lunenburg Teachers' Association,
the Massachusetts Teachers' Association and the National Reading Association.
He leaves his wife, Caroline J. (Thibodeau) Angevine; a son, Harry F. Angevine; two
daughters, Grace and Jennifer Angevine, all of Leominster; his mother, Grace Whitimore Angevine;
and a brother, James Angevine, both of Pittsburgh, Pennsylvania.
Harry Angevine was a true professional and a dedicated teacher. He will be sorely missed by
his family, his friends, his colleagues and the students of Lunenburg High School.
RETIREMENTS DURING 1985
Paul first came to Lunenburg on March 21,
1971 as a custodian at the Turkey Hill Middle
School. On June 25, 1971, he resigned to accept
another position. However, on April 18, 1972, he
returned again to the position of custodian at
the middle school, replacing a custodian who
transferred elsewhere in the system. He remained
in this position until July 26, 1985 at which time
he chose to retire after having served a total of
thirteen years.
In June, 1985, the staff tendered a farewell
party for Paul at which time a cash gift was pre-
sented to him and personnel extended their best
PAUL A. BELLAFIORE
wishes for a long and healthy retirement.
(13 years of service)
141
===PAGE 144===
"Ginny" is a graduate of Lunenburg High
School, Class of 1943. After having worked in an
office in Fitchburg for six months, she began as
Secretary to the Superintendent of Schools on
December 26, 1943. At that time, Lunenburg was
a member of Union #24 (consisting of Ashby,
Lunenburg, and Townsend). The office was
located in the Spaulding Memorial School in Town-
send. In 1951, the office moved to its présent
location (Brooks House) in Lunenburg and on
July 1, 1954, Lunenburg withdrew from the union.
The Superintendent and Ginny remained with Lun-
enburg. She served with the following superin-
tendents over her forty-two plus years of ser-
vice: Charles M. Robinson) from 1943-1949);
Maurice P. Billings (from 1949-1962); Ralph W.
Brown (from 1962-1970); Dr. William R. Holland
(from 1970-1978); and Dr. William C. Allard (from:
1978-present).
VIRGINIA FREEMAN WELCH
(42 years of service)
Some of the highlights of her tenure includ-
ed being honored in December 1973 for her thirty
years of service at a brief ceremony held in the Superintendent's office by the Lunenburg School
Committee and fellow employees. She was presented with an engraved pewter tray. In June 1977
(thirty years after her graduation), she was awarded her Gold "L" at the graduation exercises and
in October 1979, staff and students of Lunenburg High School honored her by naming her Grand
Marshal for their Homecoming activities.
On October 19, 1985, family, friends, fellow employees and staff members (past and present) as
well as school committee members (past and present), and residents of the town of Lunenburg,
honored "Ginny" for her forty-two years of service and impending retirement effective February 28,
1986, at a testimonial dinner/dance held at the Knights of Columbus in Leominster. She was pre-
sented with an executive typewriter and cash gift.
As reported by Jean Hobbs, Sentinel Correspondent, "Tributes were paid to Ginny to what some
termed as the 'cheapest assistant superintendent the town ever had' by former local superintendents
Ralph W. Brown (now retired), William R. Holland (superintendent of schools in Narragansett, R.I.)
and William C. Allard (present Lunenburg superintendent).
"Also, School Committee Chairman Judith E. Male, representing present and former school
committee members; Cynthia Daukantas, president of the Lunenburg Education Association; and
Madeline Abood, president of the Worcester County Educational Secretaries Association, spoke in
Welch's honor.
"State Senator Mary L. Padula, R-Lunenburg, Welch's high school classmate, brought greetings
from Governor Michael S. Dukakis and a citation from the Massachusetts State Senate. Richard
Garvey, faculty member at the Turkey Hill Middle School, served as master-of-ceremonies.
"In her speech at the party, Welch stated 'she was proud to be a secretary and this evening
is the frosting on the cake, culminating my 42 years. With the faith, support and encouragement
of the five superinténdents, with give and take on both parts, we made it. I will miss all the many
friends I have made over the years, both in the school and in the town. But it is time for me to
take time to smell the flowers'".
Representative Augusta Hornblower, R-Groton, in November presented a Citation to Welch on
behalf of the Massachusetts House of Representatives. In December, Lunenburg School Committee
members (past and present), presented Ginny with an engraved Revere bowl in appreciation for her
dedicated service to school committees from 1943-1985.
Ginny is a) member of the Worcester County Educational Secretaries Association and served as
its secretary from 1978-1980 and its president from 1980-1984. She is a member of the Massachusetts
Association of School Secretaries and has served as the Editor of its publication, School Daze, since
1979. This association nominated her as their selection from Massachusetts as "Office Employee of
the Year" to the National Association of Educational Office Personnel, of which Welch is also a
member.
Ginny celebrated her 37th wedding anniversary on December 31st and is looking forward to
enjoying her home in Townsend and spending more time with her husband, Bob, enjoying their
shared hobby of gardening, as well as having time for her other hobby--sewing and crafts. All
of us wish her the best--a long and healthy retirement. We will miss you, Ginny!

"Happiness exists where there is, in addition to the things that
bring satisfaction and contentment, a conscious awareness that one has
these things. Most of us have the first qualification for happiness--we have
an abundance of blessings. It is in the second area that we fall short-we fail
to recognize the fact that we have all these wonderful things. Only through such
recognition can we achieve happiness."
